#6885. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

#6880. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6879. Absolutely Everything You Need to Know About the Citizen Watch Catalog

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Japanese brand offers endless variety, appealing affordability, and incredible technological innovation.

Whether you encounter them in multi-brand boutiques and stand-alone stores, in duty-free shops while on vacation, on the Internet, or at auction, Citizen’s watches are everywhere.

Founded in 1918, the brand that began with the simple objective of producing a Japanese-made watch has evolved into a truly global juggernaut, with annual turnover of approximately $2.1 billion for the fiscal year that ended in March 2026. Vertically integrated, it produces everything from mechanical divers of the traditional ilk to tech-forward, satellite-guided modern tool watches that update time zones instantly as one zigzags across the world. Its light-powered Eco-Drive technology essentially constitutes a movement category in its own right, while its wafer-thin Eco-Drive One models—some of the brand’s highest-end wares—command luxury prices.

Like Seiko—another homegrown Japanese brand—Citizen’s considerable scale and power has compounded over more than a century. And also like Seiko, it has grown its manufacturing capabilities to such an extent that it has successfully expanded into industries other than watchmaking. But where Citizen truly shines is in offering a remarkably capable and robust product at a price that elicits few complaints from the watch-buying public. Available in points of sale in roughly 130 countries—500 alone in the U.S.—Citizen’s timepieces are not only affordable but also truly ubiquitous.

For someone in search of a relatively inexpensive watch—any type of watch, really, save perhaps a high complication in precious metal with a dial made by a lone monk living in the Swiss Jura—that will hold up under adverse conditions, Citizen is a natural answer. Whether you’re looking for a feature-laden dive watch (Promaster Aqualand), an elegant, ultrathin dress watch (Eco-Drive One), a luxurious, design-forward everyday watch (Series 8), or a vintage-inspired take on a Genta-esque design (Zenshin), there’s a good chance that there’s a Citizen timepiece that will satisfy your desires.

The only issue is parsing the firm’s gigantic catalog in search of an ideal choice. To help you in this regard, we produced this handy guide.

While not as old as Seiko, Citizen has a long and storied watchmaking history filled with a staggering amount of technological innovation. In fact, it wouldn’t be too bold to claim that in a world based primarily on centuries-old technology, Citizen is one of the most forward-thinking marques.

The brand’s story begins in 1918, when entrepreneur Kamekichi Yamazaki established the Shokosha Watch Research Institute in Tokyo with the goal of providing a domestically produced alternative to imported Swiss and American watches. By 1924, it had produced its first pocket watch, which it named “Citizen” at the behest of Tokyo mayor Count Shinpei Goto, who imagined a watch available to ordinary members of society. Six years later, the Shokosha Watch Research Institute was officially incorporated as the Citizen Watch Company and began producing wristwatches in 1931.

In 1936, the company established its Tanashi factory in Tokyo, allowing it to significantly expand its industrial capacity and begin exporting watches to Southeast Asia, China, and elsewhere. By 1941, it established a machine-tool business that allowed it to move beyond watchmaking and that still exists to this day. Postwar expansion continued at a dizzying rate, with Citizen developing shock-resistant Parashock technology in 1956 (which it demonstrated by dropping watches out of a helicopter). In 1959, it debuted the Parawater, Japan’s first fully waterproof watch, and in 1962 came the Diamond Flake, an ultrathin model equipped with a hand-wound movement.

By the 1960s, Citizen began experimenting with technologies that would come to define its place in the contemporary watch market: First, in 1966, came the X-8, the country’s first electronic wristwatch, followed in 1970 by the X-8 Chronometer, which holds the distinction of being the world’s very first titanium wristwatch. And while the brand wasn’t the first to embrace quartz technology, it did so with vigor by 1973, introducing the Crystron Mega in 1975, which was accurate to an astonishing ±3 seconds per year.

The technology that would eventually evolve into Eco-Drive—the brand’s signature system that converts ambient light into electrical energy, thus reducing the frequency of battery replacements—debuted in 1976 with the advent of its Crystron Solar Cell, the world’s first light-powered, analog-dial quartz watch. Then, in 1982, Citizen released the Professional Diver, which boasted an incredible-for-the-era 1,300 meters of water resistance; it followed up three years later with the Aqualand, the first dive watch with an onboard electronic depth gauge. In 1989, it gathered its diverse sports watch offerings together within the Promaster line.

Innovation continued throughout the 1990s, with Citizen introducing the world’s first multi-zone radio-controlled wristwatch in 1993. In 2011, it debuted the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave, a watch that synchronized with satellites to provide hyper-accurate timekeeping across different time zones. Five years later, in 2016, the Eco-Drive One hit the market with a light-powered movement just 1mm thick, building upon ultrathin offerings from the brand’s back catalog. Then, in 2019, it brought forth the Eco-Drive Caliber 0100, a movement with stunning accuracy of ±1 second per year.

Today, Citizen Group is more than just Citizen, comprising movement maker Miyota, the aforementioned precision machinery business, watch brands Bulova and Frédérique Constant, and much more. It’s a vertically integrated behemoth with more than 12,000 employees worldwide. Founded over a century ago, it’s grown into one of the most important brands within the watchmaking sphere—and beyond.

Like fellow Japanese watchmaker Seiko, Citizen produces such a dizzying array of timepieces that it’s nearly impossible to categorize them in any definitive manner. Many modern collections overlap in remit with older historical collections that are still kicking around, while certain collections are available only in certain markets. (Some frankly defy categorization entirely, and others should probably be cut from the brand’s catalog.)

What follows, therefore, is a helpful (albeit admittedly non-exhaustive) list of Citizen’s most important and recognizable product families, delineated with the understanding that certain watches—including several dedicated ladies collections from the Japanese domestic market—will invariably be left out. Still, the following rough descriptions should help orient the new (or seasoned!) collector.

The Rolan Collection is an excellent place to search for a highly affordable, versatile everyday watch. Using Citizen’s signature Eco-Drive technology, each model is easily powered by ambient light and virtually never requires a battery change. While certain references are paired to matching metal bracelets, others come on stylish straps, and all take their aesthetic inspiration from classic mid-century horological tropes. Simple and handsome, the Rolan Collection watches are perfect for someone in search of their first “nice” watch.

The ref. AW2110-06E is everything you need in a dress/everyday watch and nothing you don’t. Measuring 41mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a radially brushed black sector dial with applied indices and a luminous dauphine handset. Paired to a black leather strap with stainless-steel hardware and powered by Eco-Drive technology, it’s a timepiece that could conceivably be paired with just about anything. Moreover, at just a hair over $300, it makes a highly affordable gift for a recent graduate who needs a solid timepiece to wear on an interview or to a first job.

Notable Reference: AW2110-06EType: DressDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. J830 Eco-DrivePrice: $296.25MORE INFO HERE

The Garrison Collection contains military-inspired field watches and chronographs in a variety of styles, most of which are widely available in the United States both online and at authorized dealers. Powered by Eco-Drive technology, they’re reliable, good-looking pieces on bracelets or straps that all retail for less than $500, making them—like their counterparts in the dressier Rolan collection—excellent gifting options. For someone who’s after mil-spec looks but who doesn’t want to shell out for a vintage watch, the Garrison is a perfect choice.

The ref. BM8180-03E is ubiquitous at Citizen points of sale and neatly encapsulates the brand’s ethos. Measuring a comfortable 37mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a black dial with luminous Arabic indices, an outer five-minute track, and a day-date display at three o’clock. Powered by Eco-Drive, highly legible, and wildly affordable, it still boasts 100 meters of water resistance and a two-piece green fabric strap for less than $300. Looking like an issued military watch from the 1960s, it’s ideal for outdoor use while hiking or on vacation

Notable Reference: BM8180-03EType: FieldDiameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. E100 Eco-DrivePrice: $221.25MORE INFO HERE

Established in 1989, the Promaster Collection is where Citizen parks its most formidable tool watches. Organized into Marine, Sky, and Land subcategories, they localize many of the notable innovations that the brand developed over the past 60-plus years, from the early Parawater’s water resistance to the Aqualand’s electronic depth gauge to the Navihawk’s multifunction timekeeping and more. Today the collection encompasses everything from mechanical divers to radio-controlled pilot’s watches to exploration-oriented field watches.

Though Citizen is famous for its Eco-Drive technology, it’s worth noting that the brand has a long history of crafting hardcore mechanical tool watches. The modern Promaster Fujitsubo ref. NB6026-56L—a limited edition based upon a vintage 1977 diver found still ticking on an Australian beach in 1983—is one such timepiece. Crafted from Super Titanium with a platinum DLC coating, it features a marine-blue bezel and dial, a matching triple-link bracelet, 200 meters of water resistance, and an automatic movement with a power reserve of 42 hours.

Notable Reference: NB6026-56LType: DiverDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

Launched in 2008, discontinued in 2014, and relaunched in 2021, the Series 8 Collection is based upon the idea of an angular, eight-part case design containing a mechanical movement. Though the collection has expanded somewhat beyond its original aesthetic remit, it still emphasizes the idea of a contemporary mechanical offering with clean lines and bold geometric forms. Comprising several model families, the Series 8 features integrated-style bracelets, textured dials, and enhanced resistance to magnetic fields of up to 16,000 A/m.

Executed in a tasteful two-tone colorway, the Series 8 831 ref. NB6059-57P offers outsize value from a variety of angles. There’s its 40mm stainless-steel case with surprisingly sophisticated geometry. But there’s also its handsome Champagne dial with rose-gold-tone furniture, its caliber 9051 automatic movement, its tapered H-link bracelet with brushed and finished surfaces, and its 100 meters of water resistance. For someone looking for a handsome and versatile everyday watch that can be worn to the office or to the beach, it’s an excellent, relatively affordable option.

Notable Reference: NB6059-57PType: Everyday Diameter: 40mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,125MORE INFO HERE

Dating to 1987, the Attessa Collection focuses on the marriage of advanced materials with technologies such as Eco-Drive, radio control, and GPS timekeeping. Building upon Citizen’s innovations with titanium from 1970’s X-8 Chronometer, it offers lightweight, function-forward watches in a variety of categories. Sporty, practical, and good-looking, the collection’s tool watches ditch classically inspired looks in favor of aesthetics that balance practical functionality with sleek lines, subdued materials, and elaborate case finishing.

The Attesa F950 ref. CC4107-80H encapsulates many of the technologies that make Citizen’s wares stand out in an extremely crowded marketplace. There’s the 43.2mm Super Titanium case in dark gray DLC with recrystallized titanium accents; the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave GPS movement with time-only signal reception in under three seconds; and the multifunction display with world time, chronograph, perpetual calendar, power reserve, and numerous other features. Fueled by exposure to ambient light, it’s a futuristic tool with streaks of ’70s inspiration.

Notable Reference: CC4107-80HType: Everyday Diameter: 43.2mmMovement: Citizen cal. F950 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,195MORE INFO HERE

While Citizen has several lines that focus on mechanical watchmaking, Tsuyosa is particularly notable for combining this with stainless-steel construction, integrated-bracelet styling, colorful dials, and accessible pricing. Rather than emphasizing advanced materials or modern timekeeping tech, it uses classic 20th-century watchmaking as an inspiration, seemingly adopting the idea that simpler is better. From pastel-hued 37mm models to small-seconds designs with undulating dial patterns, it’s one of the brand’s most compelling offerings.

The Tsuyosa 37mm is truly “everything you need and nothing you don’t” in wristwatch form. Constructed from stainless steel, it features alternating brushed and polished surfaces throughout its multifaceted case and integrated multi-link bracelet, lending it a sophistication that belies its sub-$1,000 pricing. Available in multiple colors, its sunray dial with luminous indices and magnified date window is legible and direct, while its automatic caliber 8210 movement and 50 meters of water resistance offer more than enough utility for daily wear. A set-and-forget watch.

Notable Reference: NJ0200-50MType: Everyday Diameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8210 automaticPrice: $371.25MORE INFO HERE

The Zenshin Collection brings together several compelling features in a single product family: Super Titanium (titanium construction with Duratect surface-hardening treatments), automatic or Eco-Drive movements, angular cases with integrated bracelets inspired by Citizen’s 1980s output, textured dials, sapphire crystals, 100 meters of water resistance, and relatively accessible pricing. Available in three-hand and chronograph configurations, Zenshin brings a bit more materials science than Tsuyosa but costs much less than comparable Attesa fare.

Though a 42.4mm chronograph might smack of early-2000s excess, Super Titanium construction and a comfortable integrated bracelet help keep the Zenshin Chrono under control on the wrist. Available in several colorways, the “panda” colorway ref. CA4610-85A with a textured white-silver dial and contrasting black outer tachymeter scale and chronograph registers is particularly attractive and versatile. Powered by Eco-Drive, it features a 24-hour display as well as a date window—and its sub-$600 price is tough to argue with.

Notable Reference: CA4610-85AType: Everyday Diameter: 42.4mmMovement: Citizen cal. B620 Eco-DrivePrice: $506.25MORE INFO HERE

The Citizen is Citizen’s purest expression of horological excellence. Building upon the firm’s legacy of highly accurate mechanical timekeeping in the mid-century Chronomaster Collection, it launched the collection in 1995 to emphasize its commitment to precision, simplicity, and longevity. Today it comprises both mechanical and Eco-Drive movements, marrying them with traditional Japanese craftsmanship in the form of washi paper dials, hand-dyed indigo treatments, and other decorative techniques. Wildly accurate, they’re among Citizen’s most expensive offerings.

If you’re the type of person who values accuracy above all else in a wristwatch—but wouldn’t mind great looks thrown in for good measure—then chances are the Citizen ref. AQ4100-65X might be the timepiece for you. Measuring 38.3mm in the brand’s Super Titanium, it’s powered by the Eco-Drive caliber A060, which is accurate to ±5 seconds per year. (And that innocuous-seeming date window? It’s actually a perpetual date window that doesn’t need manual updating.) Oh, and the red dial? Ultra-thin Tosa Tengujoshi paper. (Hence the price.)

Notable Reference: AQ4100-65XType: Dress/everyday Diameter: 38.3mmMovement: Citizen cal. A060 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,995MORE INFO HERE

If the Citizen Collection is all about accuracy, then the Eco-Drive One Collection is about combining that accuracy—achieved via the brand’s signature Eco-Drive technology—with mind-bogglingly thin case architecture and precision manufacturing. Launched in 2016 to coincide with the 40th anniversary of Eco-Drive, the lineup features several watches powered by the caliber 8826, which was introduced as the world’s thinnest light-powered movement at just 1mm tall. When it’s combined with Super Titanium and other advanced tech, these pieces are truly remarkable.

Not only is the Eco-Drive One a spectacularly thin timepiece at just 4.5mm tall, but it’s also a remarkably handsome dress watch. Measuring 36.6mm wide in stainless steel, it features a flat gray dial with a contrasting dark-gray minute track, applied gold-tone indices with a matching dauphine handset, and a dark-gray sub-seconds display above six o’clock. That its crown is tiny shouldn’t be much of a concern—the caliber 8845 Eco-Drive is so accurate that you’ll hardly ever need to adjust it. (Unless you change time zones, that is.) Truly a one-and-done dress watch.

Notable Reference: AQ5010-01AType: DressDiameter: 36.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8845 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,395MORE INFO HERE

A limited-edition offering celebrating 50 years of Eco-Drive technology, the Eco-Drive Photon Collection combines highly geometric case shapes with dials inspired by the physics of light transmission. Currently available in two references, each features a slit dial that dynamically shifts in color as light interacts with its surface. Paired with integrated bracelets and measuring 39.6mm in Super Titanium, they’re more premium offerings than many Citizen core-catalog models but significantly less expensive than Eco-Drive One or Citizen watches.

A distinctive, contemporary take on the luxury sports watch, the Eco-Drive Photon ref. BJ6569-59X combines dark-gray and amber-yellow Super Titanium case construction to eye-catching effect. The three-dimensional dial with overlapping slits creates even more visual interest, while the caliber E036 Eco-Drive movement turns any light source into a consistent source of accurate timekeeping. Water resistant to 50 meters, the Photon is an excellent everyday option for someone who appreciates a bit of Genta-inflected styling in their wardrobe.

#6877. Here’s How Much the Mirrorball Trophy Is Worth on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

We’re just a few weeks out from the Dancing With the Stars season 35 finale (it goes down on November 24), which means one couple is about to walk away with the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy. You know, the giant disco ball that everyone hoists above their heads after winning?

And if you’re wondering how much it’s worth, the answer is PRICELESS, obviously. But also, kind of a lot.

The mirrorball itself is incredibly cheap. Production designer James Yarnell told People in 2017, “No matter how much we put into the trophy, how much we spend, it always has a $10 mirrorball on top.” The show does throw down for the base, however. Winner Kaitlyn Bristowe told Trading Secrets that, all together, the trophy is worth around $10,000.

Apparently Hollywood 3D Printing—who uses “3D printing technologies combined with advanced finishing capabilities such as detailed painting, electroplating, and vacuum plating”—is responsible for making the Mirrorball Trophies, which they confirmed on Instagram in 2023.

Meanwhile, this glitzy statue has come a long way since the early days of Dancing With the Stars. Yarnell said he made the original Mirrorball Trophy (which Kelly Monaco won) from a brass lamp, a wooden base, and some tape. And it stayed that way for four full seasons! Per Yarnell, “They stopped making the lamp at Lamps Plus, so we’d run out of options and had to start casting it and put a heavy acrylic base with a brass stem.”

As for whether or not celebs and pros keep their trophies, it presumably depends on the person. But Derek Hough is so attached to his collection that he literally travels with them on tour and puts them in the lobby for fans to enjoy.

#6875. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6869. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6868. Just Turn the Heat On? That Might Be Why Your Skin Suddenly Feels So Dry

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

A few weeks ago, I rented a big cabin outside of the city with friends. It was the first cold weekend in New York, the kind where you might actually need to throw on the heavy jackets that were too much for those 55-degree fall days. Nestled in this mountain house, we cozied up around a fire in fleece sweatshirts and fuzzy blankets. Before bed, we turned the heat on. I slept cozy like a baby in the womb, but immediately upon waking, my skin felt tight and dry. I coated myself in moisturizer, but it was no use. My face—which is usually oily and acne-prone—was dull and rough, like it’d never produced a drop of oil in its 29 years.

At first, I thought it was a sign of the times. I’m about to turn 30, and my skin’s just getting drier. But when I headed back to Brooklyn, where my stuffy apartment produces enough natural heat on its own to not need the radiator cranked up, my skin went back to normal.

I’ve heard of this phenomenon before—when your skin gets dehydrated once you turn on the heat in your house—but this was my first experience. And I was determined to get to the bottom of it before my radiator goes on for good and the winter uglies take over. Two dermatologists weighed in—and it looks like this isn’t necessarily preventable but totally treatable.

“Indoor heating lowers the relative humidity in the air, which makes moisture evaporate from your skin more quickly,” says dermatologist Annette Czernik, MD. “Our skin naturally produces oils that help protect it and keep moisture in, but sometimes that's not enough when the air is really dry.” You might apply moisturizer and feel like you didn’t even do anything, she adds.

You’re also not always in the clear if you have oily skin, says Dr. Czernik. “A lot of people don't realize that your skin can be oily and dehydrated at the same time,” she explains. “This is because your skin can still produce oil while losing moisture from the dry air.”

You may also feel tightness, roughness, stinging, flaking, and even itching. “As your skin loses more moisture, its protective barrier can become weaker,” says Dr. Czernik, hence why you can experience some irritation as your skin isn’t as strong enough to fight it off. The air can also make your skin look dull or even feel more sensitive, since it’s working so hard to hold onto the moisture it needs, explains Dr. Czernik. “In more severe cases, you might experience redness, peeling, or even small cracks in your skin,” she adds.

Naturally, with colder air outside, your skin might get drier too, only exacerbating the issue. “When the temperature drops outside, the air usually holds less moisture,” says Dr. Czernik. “Then, when you go indoors, you're exposed to heated air that can also be very dry.” You’re basically losing moisture on both sides, no matter what you do. “Going back and forth between the cold outdoors and warm indoors can make that dryness more noticeable,” she adds. “This is why someone might have perfectly normal skin during the summer but struggle with dryness all winter.”

“When you turn the heat on, the air inside your home can become very dry,” explains dermatologist Ariel Ostad, MD. And one of the easiest ways to add moisture back into the air is a humidifier. “This can help slow down how quickly your skin loses water throughout the day or night,” Dr. Ostad adds.

He recommends getting a bedside humidifier, like the Canopy Bedside Humidifier, for your bedroom and running it while you’re sleeping. “You may notice that your skin feels less tight and dry when you wake up in the morning,” he says. “It can also help with dry lips and irritated skin.” But remember to clean it regularly, as bacteria and mold can build up in your device.

“If your skin starts getting drier, I would first look at the products you're using every day,” says Dr. Ostad. Both Dr. Ostad and Dr. Czernik recommend switching to a thicker moisturizer. Ideally, find one that both moisturizes (meaning it contains ingredients that pull water into your skin, like squalane, hyaluronic acid, panthenol, and glycerin) and seals in hydration (aka ingredients that act as a physical barrier to reduce moisture loss, including ceramides, oils, butters, and silicones). But you could also opt for two moisturizers nightly. I prefer to use a lightweight moisturizer, then follow it up with a barrier cream.

“It's also a good idea to apply your moisturizer right after washing your face or getting out of the shower,” adds Dr. Czernik. If your skin is still damp, even better. This helps pull all that water into your skin (which helps plump it up and decrease dullness), plus it eliminates time for moisture to evaporate from your skin.

Just as hot air can pull water from your skin, so can hot water, explains Dr. Czernik. It doesn’t mean you need to do a daily cold plunge, but opt for mild to warm water temperatures, as scalding water can also increase skin irritation.

Some of the products in your routine could actually be making your dryness worse. Put down all the strong exfoliants, whether it’s your salicylic acid cleanser, an acid-based peel pad, or a retinol serum.

Instead, Dr. Ostad suggests using a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser “that won't strip your skin of its natural oils.” And if you love your exfoliants, you don’t have to ditch them completely, he adds, but you might want to cut back to once or twice per week at most. “You don't need to completely change your skincare routine, but making a few small adjustments can really help during the colder months,” he says.

“If your skin is just a little dry, you can usually manage it at home with a good moisturizer,” says Dr. Ostad. “However, if the dryness continues for a few weeks despite changing your skincare routine, I would recommend seeing a dermatologist.” If you notice your skin becomes extremely itchy or irritated, that’s a good sign to see a doctor too. “Cracking, bleeding, or painful peeling are signs that something more serious could be going on,” adds Dr. Ostad.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

#6867. Kings of Leon’s Caleb Followill Is Finally Ready to Bare It All

The lead singer tells Esquire in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned” that the band’s new record features its most honest music to date.

Last year, Kings of Leon was forced to cancel part of its summer tour. The band’s lead singer, Caleb Followill, broke his foot and severely displaced his heel during a freak accident that he jokes was “a rock ’n’ roll injury.” But really, he admits to Esquire, he was just playing tag with his kids—a little roughhousing that turned into eight months of recovery before he could walk back onto the stage.

“I was kind of feeling sorry for myself,” Followill tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The singer felt like he had let the band’s fans down. He turned 44, he lost his mother not too long ago, and he channeled all that grief and negative energy into a new, stripped-down Kings of Leon record, O My Beloved, which was released on November 6. “I had a weight on my shoulders,” he says. “And one day I picked up the guitar, and it was almost like I walked through a door and I could see things a little more clearly.”

O My Beloved is the most melancholic and reflective album in Kings of Leon’s ten-album discography. Followill tells Esquire that he wanted to speak about his childhood and the family that shaped him on the new songs because he used to feel embarrassed about his past. His family grew up poor—traveling around the South and living out of their car at times. (Kings of Leon is a family band, with Caleb joined by his brothers Nathan and Jared Followill, as well as their cousin Matthew Followill.) But now Caleb wants to pass all his memories on to his kids, and he doesn’t want them to feel ashamed about any part of the journey that led them here.

“When I was writing these songs, my mind just kept going back to small moments in my life—moments that had no significance,” he says. “I wanted to tell these stories, and I wanted to be honest about parts of my life that I didn’t want to be honest about before."

O My Beloved is the band’s first self-produced record since parting ways with RCA, its longtime record label. It’s also the band’s tenth studio album, celebrating nearly 20 years since the 2008 record Only by the Night rocketed it to stardom thanks to hit songs “Sex on Fire” and “Use Somebody.”

“I was always scared of fame, and I think I still am,” Followill says. “That’s the good thing about having your brothers next to you. They’ll rip you right back down, shine a light in your eyes, and say, ‘Hey man, this can all come crumbling down and we’ll be back in our crumbling house.’ ”

You can listen to the episode on YouTube and Spotify. Elsewhere, Followill shares his secret to 15 years of marriage (to model Lily Aldridge) and what he’s learned from touring with Pearl Jam, U2, and Zach Bryan.

#6866. Designers Reveal the Fastest Way They’ve Updated a Living Room Under $100

A few small swaps can make your space feel brand new.

A living room is arguably the most popular spot of the house, so why not give it the tender loving care it deserves with a design refresh? Problem is, when it comes to most home projects, it’s easy to convince yourself good things only come to those who wait—or anyone willing to fork over a small fortune.

Fortunately, affordability and efficiency are no longer mutually exclusive. According to the pros, it’s possible to elevate a space swiftly and without spending much money. Want proof? Keep scrolling for the clever (and quick) ways designers have upgraded a living room for $100 or less.

Barbra Fordyce of Ginger Lemon Indigo says plants are “hands-down” one of the best low-cost, high-impact ways to spruce up your space. “Bringing something living into your living room instantly changes the mood, quite literally bringing life, nature and an organic beauty into the room,” explains the designer. While fresh foliage can look good anywhere, she loves how plants can add a “verdant richness” to neutral rooms without throwing off the entire color palette.

That said, houseplants aren’t a one-size-fits-all situation, so choose wisely.

“If you have high ceilings, you want the plant to be proportional to those dimensions,” Fordyce says. “If the plant or tree is too small in a large living room, it will feel dwarfed.”

For the perfect finishing touch, use your leftover budget to buy a beautiful planter. “The container should echo the feeling of your room,” she shares. “A basket, ceramic container or even a vintage wooden crate can all make beautiful ‘pots’ for your plants.”

Though there is such a thing as too many throw pillows, the right ones can make a difference. “A pillow and throw refresh can go a long way in bringing new life to your sofa,” explains Natalie Kaelin, an interior designer in Raleigh, North Carolina.

Of course, there’s no need to buy brand-new lumbar and square cushions on a whim: Simply purchase covers for your existing pillows and swap them out seasonally.

Neutral linen layers can conjure coastal grandmother vibes virtually anywhere, while plush, jewel-toned velvets offer an elegant, autumnal look. Come winter, embrace the holiday spirit with flannel fabric or a plaid repeat.

We might spend a lot of time talking about layered lighting or how to pick the perfect fixture, but we cannot stress it enough: Pay attention to your bulbs.

“Daylight LED bulbs are incredibly white, so they put out light akin to doctors’ offices and hospitals,” says Elizabeth Cross-Beard of Palm Henri in Baltimore. “A warmer temperate bulb gives a home a candle-light, cozy feel.” As a general rule of thumb, she recommends buying lightbulbs with a temperature of 2700K and avoiding anything above 4000K.

Cross-Beard says switching your bulbs can effectively “redesign” your space, and it’s shockingly inexpensive. “Depending on how many lights you have, it can cost as little as $50,” she adds.

If you want to upgrade your space without spending a cent, Blair Burton says to start with your furniture.

“Sometimes simply pulling furniture away from the walls and creating a more comfortable conversation area can completely change how a room feels,” the Austin-based designer explains.

After all, just because an armchair has been in the corner of your living room for years doesn’t mean it needs to go there. By doing a room rearrangement, you might end up using it more when it’s in front of a warm, sun-drenched window. Maybe you’ll even realize you need to pick up an ottoman to create the ultimate cozy space.

Another $0 hack? Burton also encourages her clients to “shop their homes.” “It’s practical, of course, but it's also about honoring the things they've collected and loved over the years,” she explains. “Sometimes finding an unexpected new home for a favorite piece is one of the most satisfying discoveries in a project.”

“Adding artwork, whether it's a gallery wall, a single large-scale piece, or something in between, makes a huge visual and personal impact in a living room,” says Molly Torres Portnof of DATE Interiors in New York.

Retailers like Minted and Society6 offer well-priced prints, but you can also reframe photographs and posters you already own. As long as you pay attention to scale and proportion, the ceiling’s really the limit. “Rules are meant to be broken,” Portnof says. “If it feels right and makes you happy, you're on the right track.”

If your living room is blessed with beautiful built-ins, you can fill them with treasures you already own, or buy a few new pieces for well under $100.

“Take the time to create vignettes,” advises Tennessee designer Katie Kiser. “More often than not, I find my clients have all the pieces for a well-choreographed space, but they're styled improperly.”

For an instant yet affordable upgrade, head over to your local antique store to pick up some art books, jars, or a trinket tray.

#6854. The Ultimate Guide to the Rolex Daytona, the World’s Most Wanted Watch

From the hand-wound watches dealers struggled to sell to the steel model that commands an impressive premium today.

In September, four Rolex Daytonas sold at a charity auction in London for a combined £37.3 million (about $49.5 million). The most expensive, at £10.3 ($13.7) million, had a turquoise dial and a ring of pink sapphires. That same month, Rolex unveiled a steel-and-platinum Daytona with a white enamel dial and a speed scale that recalled the original from 1963.

The regular steel Daytona with a white dial costs $16,900 from Rolex, if you can get one. In September, WatchCharts put its secondary-market value around $35,000. It wasn’t always like this. In its early years, according to Sotheby’s, retailers discounted Daytonas to clear stock.

Rolex launched the Cosmograph in 1963. An early advert called it the “Le Mans,” after the French endurance race. The name that stuck came from Daytona, Florida, where cars raced on hard-packed sand before the city had a speedway.

The first model, reference 6239, had a movement you wound by hand and two buttons to operate the stopwatch. Rolex put the speed scale on the steel bezel, where a driver could read it at a glance. The three small counters stood out against the dial, black on a light face or light on a black one.

By the 1970s, two models had emerged that collectors still prize today. The 6263 had a black acrylic bezel, and the 6265 had a metal one. Both had screw-down collars around the stopwatch buttons. On some, the word Daytona curves in red above the bottom counter. Collectors call these “Big Reds.”

Joanne Woodward gave Newman a 6239 around the time he made Winning, the 1969 film that sparked his interest in racing. She had “DRIVE CAREFULLY ME” engraved on the back. “There’s something almost magical about it,” James Cox, who later owned the watch, told Phillips. In 2017 it sold for $17.75 million, then an auction record for a wristwatch.

A “Paul Newman” usually refers to a type of dial. Look for the unusual numerals and small square-ended markers in the counters. The dials were unpopular when new. By 2013, Christie’s could sell 50 Daytonas in a single Geneva auction for $13.2 million. The top lot was a steel watch with a Paul Newman dial. It fetched $1.09 million, four years before the actor’s own watch went to auction.

On one particularly rare black-dial 6263, the word Oyster appears below Rolex Cosmograph. Collectors call it the Oyster Sotto, from the Italian for under. Phillips sold one in 2025 for 1.39 million Swiss francs (approximately $1.67 million). “One mistake people make is that they expect everything to fit neatly into a specific set of rules,” Geoff Hess, global head of watches at Sotheby’s, once explained. A dial can be genuine Rolex and still have come from a different watch.

In 1988 the steel Daytona became the 16520. It grew to 40mm, gained raised gold hour markers and a glossier dial, and wound itself as you wore it. Rolex extensively reworked Zenith’s El Primero movement for the watch. It was the first automatic Daytona and the model that established the basic dimensions of the watch Rolex still makes today.

On some early examples, a gap makes the word Cosmograph appear to float beneath the rest of the lettering. On certain black dials, the rings around the counters have turned brown with age. Those are known as Patrizzi dials, after the auctioneer Osvaldo Patrizzi, who noticed the change.

Three steel references are worth knowing. The 116520, introduced in 2000, has a steel bezel and the first chronograph movement Rolex developed and made itself. The 116500LN, from 2016, brought back the look of the black acrylic bezel in ceramic. The current 126500LN, introduced in 2023, has a slim steel rim around that ceramic bezel, a revised dial, and a new movement that runs for about three days when fully wound. The two ceramic models can look remarkably similar in listing photographs.

The unicorn is a hand-wound 6265 that Phillips described in 2018 as the only known vintage Daytona in white gold. It sold for 5.94 million Swiss francs (about $7.1 million). The Rainbow gets its name from the graduated sapphires around its bezel. A white-gold example made around 1994, nearly two decades before the production versions appeared, sold in 2024 for 5.505 million Swiss francs (approximately $6.1 million).

Sixty years after that early advertisement, Rolex returned to Le Mans. Its 2023 watch for the race’s centenary has a stopwatch that counts the full 24 hours. The regular Daytona counts to 12.

An official Rolex retailer is the place to start if you want a new one at list price. Retailers manage their own allocations, so availability varies. Rolex addressed the shortage in 2021. “The scarcity of our products is not a strategy on our part,” it said, explaining that it could not meet demand without compromising quality.

On the secondary market, compare the same reference and dial. The WatchCharts September estimate for the current black-dial steel model was about $29,000, roughly $6,000 less than the white. If you’re buying vintage, ask a specialist dealer or auction house for a condition report. Find out whether the case has been heavily polished, which parts have been replaced, and whether the dial is correct for that particular watch. On a Daytona, those details can explain more about the price than the reference number.

#6850. ‘Rivals’ Season 2’s Shocking Tony Baddingham Scene, Explained—And What Happens Next

David Tennant’s ruthless TV boss has officially reached breaking point.

Warning: Major spoilers for Rivals season 2, part 2 ahead.

Just when we thought things couldn't get any more dramatic in Rutshire, Rivals has delivered one of its most shocking moments yet. The second half of season 2 has finally landed on Disney+ and Hulu, picking up in the aftermath of Lady Monica Baddingham's (Claire Rushbrook) devastating death. And while her husband Tony (David Tennant) is struggling to come to terms with losing his wife, his grief is taking a seriously disturbing turn.

Because, in a scene that nobody saw coming, the ruthless Corinium Television boss takes a rifle to Monica's beloved peacocks. Yes, you read that correctly.

It's a particularly brutal moment, even by Tony's standards, and one that reveals just how far he's spiraling following his wife's death.

But what exactly pushed him to breaking point? And what does it mean for the rest of the season?

When we last visited Rutshire, Monica had been killed in a car accident after discovering that Tony was having an affair with Maud O'Hara (Victoria Smurfit).

The latest episodes pick up in the aftermath of her death, with Tony struggling to process his grief while facing the consequences of his infidelity.

While the TV boss has never exactly been known for his kindness, the scene takes his cruelty to another level.

The peacocks are a reminder of Monica and the life they shared, making his decision to kill them feel particularly vicious.

Rather than dealing with the pain of losing his wife, Tony appears to direct his anger toward something she loved.

Speaking to Deadline about the latest episodes, David and executive producer Dominic Treadwell-Collins discussed Tony's increasingly destructive behavior following Monica's death.

He told the outlet: "To lose your life partner is devastating. Even for someone who’s not always the most morally engaged human, I think it’s bewildering and it’s distressing for him. He’s rudderless without her, so I think it’s quite a plausible reaction.

"He’s a man who doesn’t have a particularly well-oiled path to his own emotions, so I think the fact that such a traumatic moment should end with some kind of violent exorcism makes perfect sense. I think that’s who Tony is, unfortunately."

For someone who has spent much of the series manipulating those around him, Tony suddenly finds himself unable to control the one thing that matters most: his own life.

Of course, there's also the small matter of his affair with Maud, which continues to complicate an already messy situation.

If you were hoping Monica's death might inspire Tony to turn over a new leaf, we wouldn't hold our breath.

Despite everything that's happened, Tony remains determined to maintain his grip on Corinium Television, with his bitter rivalry against Declan O'Hara (Aidan Turner) showing no signs of slowing down.

And things are about to get even more complicated now that Declan has discovered the truth about Tony's affair with Maud.

The revelation leads to an explosive confrontation during the Bonfire Night celebrations, leaving Declan and Maud's marriage hanging in the balance.

Meanwhile, Tony's relationship with his son Archie remains fractured, and his increasingly unpredictable behavior raises questions about just how far he's prepared to go to protect his position.

With the battle for the television franchise reaching a critical point, Tony could find himself facing consequences both professionally and personally.

And given what we've just witnessed, we're slightly terrified to see what he does next.

The first three episodes of Rivals season 2, part 2 are available to stream on Disney+ and Hulu, with the remaining episodes arriving weekly.

#6846. Designers Say This High-Gloss Paint Trick Can Make Your Kitchen Look Instantly More Expensive

Pictured above: This New York City kitchen by designer Francis Nicdao features a high-gloss-painted island.

Once you’ve pinpointed the ideal color palette for your kitchen, you might think that the work is done. But not so fast. The success of your shades really comes down to finish, and there’s real potential in choosing a high-gloss paint. After all, if your kitchen is the showpiece of your home, you should make it shimmer.

“There is a richness to high-gloss paint that really can't be achieved with any other finish or product, especially in deeper colors,” says Malorie Goldberg, a principal designer at Noa Blake Design. “A high-gloss finish adds interest to cabinets that makes them feel more like furniture, immediately elevating the overall feel of the space.”

This option might be tough to imagine, given that kitchens are typically painted in a matte or eggshell finish. But that doesn’t mean that a shinier upgrade is out of the question. “High-gloss kitchens can be in a small or large space. For a smaller space, a butler's pantry might be a great place to play,” notes Danielle Davis, founder and principal designer of 56th Street Design Studio. “A larger kitchen will likely have a lot of millwork, so high-gloss paint can be applied to surfaces where it can really shine.”

If you want to make a statement in your kitchen using the budget-friendly quality of paint, a high-gloss finish could be the right move. Here’s how the pros recommend pulling it off.

When you’re considering a high-gloss finish, think of how it can be used to complement the rest of your kitchen. If you’re leaning toward trending darker palettes—like chocolate browns, emerald greens, and deep plums—then a high gloss will help bounce light around the room and make undertones more noticeable throughout the day. But if you’re doing just fine with a neutral backdrop, then a high-gloss will only make that look brighter. Either way, “high gloss” is paint speak for highlighter.

“The way that light reflects off a high-gloss finish adds a brightness that can make the space feel more expansive, even in moodier tones,” says Ariel Fischer, who is also a principal at Noa Blake Design.

Fischer and Goldberg would likely pick a darker shade on kitchen cabinetry for this effect, which Goldberg notes works on ultramodern panels and more ornate fronts. But Jen Levy, interior designer at Surrounded by Color, says it’s OK to go lighter—while looking up. “I love a high gloss ceiling in a kitchen,” Levy says. “My team and I see it used either way, but my preference is to have this paint treatment punctuate a lighter, brighter space.”

Paint is often regarded as DIY-friendly, but choosing this finish probably shouldn’t involve you rolling up your sleeves. “Hire a professional,” Levy says. “High gloss involves a lot of prep work to look right.” Since the results should almost resemble a mirror, Davis echoes this advice. “If a surface isn't perfectly sanded or smooth, high-gloss will show those imperfections,” she says. “It will ultimately take away from that perfect finish you’re after.”

As far as the best location for high-gloss paint, Davis has one strong preference. “High-gloss cabinets, from a practical standpoint, are forgiving in terms of wear,” she says. “You can easily wipe fingerprints, spills, and food right off the cabinet with a microfiber cloth.” Keep in mind, though, that chips will be noticeable, so this probably isn’t the right finish for busy households with young children.

As you determine the remaining details of your kitchen with a high-gloss paint in mind, remember to keep it front and center. Everything else in this space has to complement that sheen in order for the effect to be successful. “In a rustic kitchen, or one where natural wood is prevalent, high-gloss paint may clash with the overall vibe,” Davis says. The opposite is also true: A modern kitchen with too many shiny surfaces will feel like a carnival fun house.

To pull your kitchen together, seek out countertops and flooring with a matte finish alongside lighting and hardware that has a slight sheen. The goal is to find ways to ground the room, even as you center its high-gloss boldness. “I would love to have a client who’s daring enough to do a high-gloss kitchen,” Davis says. “Nevertheless, high-gloss paint can still have a timeless quality, while still having a big impact on the room.”

#6837. Friday the 13th Just Got a Little Luckier This Year

And you can thank Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde finally coming to an end for that!

Friday the 13th may be tomorrow, but that doesn’t mean that luck isn’t in the air! This year on the longtime “unlucky” day, both Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde (finally!) come to an end within a few hours of each other, bringing relief and clarity to your close relationships and ability to express yourself.

One retrograde is painful and frustrating enough, but over the past few weeks, you’ve navigated two at the same time, which can feel immobilizing at best and chaotic at worst. Fortunately, you’re reaching the finish line! Beyond the retrograde relief, Friday the 13th is actually considered an incredibly magical day, brimming with intuitive breakthroughs, despite its reputation.

For millennia, the number 13 was actually revered by many cultures and traditions all across the globe because of its connection to the average number of lunations we experience every year and also the average number of menstrual cycles a person experiences in a year. It was celebrated as a day to connect with and honor the Goddess. People who have periods are said to experience heightened intuition during their cycle and a strong connection to the other Realms. It’s through these associations that the number 13 carries deep and profound ties to magic, creation, manifestation, and birthing both new life and new desires and visions for your future.

Over centuries, the original meaning was distorted by patriarchal leaders and religions out of fear of collective feminine energy. By concocting false narratives and creating fearful myths about Friday the 13th, the patriarchal forces were able to exercise more control all while divorcing and dispelling many feminine people from reclaiming their own power and agency. Which is actually why many people consider this an “unlucky” day—but it’s really not at all! Once again, the patriarchy was out here ruining everything.

So clear your schedule because here come the divine downloads and revelations you’ve been waiting for. With Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde ending, you’re ready to decide who and what from your past is worth clinging onto and which coffins you’re ready to put a final nail into. Here’s how this energy will manifest and how to make the most of it, based on your zodiac sign.

You’re done dealing with a lack of reciprocity in your connections. Whether it’s a current flame, ex, or BFF, it’s time for you to speak your truth and receive what you dole out so effortlessly to others. Your ability to be vulnerable and share your needs honestly can strengthen and deepen a connection in your life or protect you from someone who doesn’t deserve another ounce of your energy. There may also be a new contract or positive financial development on the horizon.

It’s time to prioritize your peace and set any necessary boundaries. Your daily commitments and responsibilities are just that—yours—and you can’t keep cleaning up other people’s messes. The end of the retrogrades ask you to confront close relationships in your life where you feel like your generosity is being taken advantage of. Standing up for yourself can bring more support and balance to your daily life as a result.

You deserve more joy in your daily life and you’re in a season of saying yes to whatever and whoever makes you happy. No one benefits from you staying so busy you can’t even hear your own thoughts in your head. The end of these retrogrades want you to reconnect with your inner child or inner teen who always made time for their creative hobbies, romantic connections, and had a zest for life—even on a Tuesday! You might experience a breakthrough in a romantic relationship or feel recommitted to a creative endeavor that fills your weeks with more passion and playfulness.

You’ve experienced healing in a close, private relationship. Maybe your sense of security in a relationship with a lover, family member, or roommate feels renewed and strengthened. You’ve reconnected with some of your own joy and found a lighter heart in the process that’s helped you understand and extend forgiveness to past situations and people that have caused hurt. You might also experience a creative renaissance, feeling inspired and confident enough to pursue who and what lights you up and that also offers security and grounding in your life, too.

You spoke your truth and are experiencing the ripple effects! Communication and self-expression are major themes for you as the retrograde ends, but especially in close relationships with chosen family, siblings, BFFs, and extended family members. There may be issues from the past that you’ve finally exposed, bringing relief, realizations and accountability in the process. Remaining silent to keep the peace benefits no one. There may also be a renewed sense of optimism in contracts or commitments that add to your sense of emotional or financial security.

You are practicing what you preach. There may have been moments in the past where your actions didn’t align with your personal values and, as a result, you felt tinges of imposter syndrome or shame. As the retrogrades end, you’ve reconnected with the power of your voice and aren’t willing to compromise what matters most to you so others can remain comfortable. Delayed contracts and negotiations also begin to pick up speed once again. You might also learn positive news that boosts your confidence or helps you invest in yourself or an idea you’re excited about.

This retrograde has been particularly trying for you and might have felt like a mini identity crisis. There’s no use in denying yourself pleasure any longer and you’re ready to debut a new, more authentic you to the world. You might also set new personal goals for yourself that feel like they shape your identity in some way—like pivoting and changing career directions or boldly addressing conflict and disagreements in a new way. You went through hell and back and you’ve finally reached your glow up—embrace it, take up space, and celebrate yourself along the way.

You’re more aware of who and what have been holding you back. As the retrogrades wrap up, you might understand some of your habits and patterns that have been self-fulfilling prophecies. You’ve engaged with your subconscious in a new way that’s helped you break certain patterns and kick limiting beliefs to the curb. You spent time with the emotions that drudge up past pain and grief. You spent many evenings crying on the bathroom floor. Now, you’ve done the inner work necessary to allow new growth and momentum to propel you toward the life of your dreams. A fresh start is around the corner.

You’re more aware of who is in your corner and who might not have your best interests at heart. The retrograde asked you to inspect and reflect on your friendships and sense of belonging. You took inventory of who you spend your time with and, not only how they made you feel, but also if your values align. Some friendships may wrap up while others are fortified. You may have also had a realization about your own self-defeating habits that are keeping you tethered to an old version of your life. You know what steps you need to take to reach your goals and dreams and the time to act is now.

You’ve felt directionless and the inability to act is so unfamiliar it felt overwhelming. The uncertainty about where you’re headed next is clearing and you can finally see the path forward. You might be ready to change career directions, accept a promotion, or finally start seeing yourself how others see you. There could have been certain people or friendships in your life that kept you in a cycle of seeking approval. But this retrograde helped you break free from living under other people’s expectations for your life and your joy. As you feel more centered and confident about who and what you want to prioritize in your life, you’re also ready to make meaningful progress on some of your longterm goals.

You felt unsure and maybe a little stuck, and you responded accordingly! There may have been a nudge or tug to go back to school, book travel to a place you’ve loved, or to hone a skill you used to enjoy. Picking up where you left off on a project and investing your mental energy in new (or old!) ways were just what you needed to feel inspired once again. There may also be certain conversations you’ve avoided with important figures in your life and, now, you feel ready to speak your truth. Your ability to speak about what’s been on your heart and mind will guide you toward the next step on your path—they are intrinsically interconnected.

You’re ready to invest in your future and take the next step. Maybe you’re ready to go back to school, sign up for a certification course, or write your memoir! You are more aware of how limited and precious your time and energy are, and how important it is to invest these precious resources into who and what makes you feel most alive. Vulnerable and healing conversations may also be a focus, asking you to navigate trust and intimacy in new ways that will either deepen bonds or ask you to move on in separate directions.

#6832. This London Townhouse Was Designed for the Holidays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Miles Redd and David Kaihoi created the ultimate English Christmas retreat for longtime clients—cozy, festive, and swathed in reds and greens.

Above: In the living room, the seating is by Luther Quintana with Samuel & Sons fringe. The wallcovering is by Iksel, and the cocktail table has a Fornasetti top.

Some homes are for summers by the water, others for weekends on the slopes. When Krystyna Houser set about redecorating her family’s London townhouse, she was thinking of Christmas. “We spend every Christmas there,” she says. At this house, that meant more than just putting up a tree.

Located near Richmond Park, in southwestern London, the 19th-century townhouse has been in Houser’s family for more than 20 years. It was always a place to return to while Houser, founder of the nutritional startup mBIOTA Elemental, and her husband Dan, a co-founder of Rockstar Games, were raising their three children in New York City. Dan had grown up nearby, and the ample greenspace that gives the area its name made it an appealing place to visit with a young family. The interiors, however, had never quite received their due.

Houser turned to Miles Redd and David Kaihoi, the partners behind the New York–based firm Redd Kaihoi. She had known Redd since 2012, when she hired him to decorate a Brooklyn Heights townhouse, and the two became friends. They have since worked on multiple houses together. For London she trusted the designers to craft the warm and celebratory atmosphere she craved. “I wanted it to be equally elegant, cozy, and family-oriented,” she says.

Red and green were a natural starting point, as green already had the family’s allegiance. “It’s one of their favorite colors,” Redd says. “So we leaned into that a lot.” Verdant hues appear in velvet, felt, and marble throughout, while the entrance hall is dressed in crimson. These were holiday colors with no intention of coming down in January, and Redd was going for sophistication rather than holiday kitsch. “I wanted romantic 19th-century elegance, the feeling that they’re stepping into a Merchant Ivory movie.”

This approach suits a firm known for exuberant color and layered furnishings. Redd brings a sense of theater to traditional decorating, drawing on the work of such legendary designers as Billy Baldwin, Albert Hadley, and John Fowler and mixing lacquer, chintz, and bold geometric patterns. Before opening his practice in 1998, he trained with antiques dealer John Rosselli and decorator Bunny Williams. Kaihoi, a sculptor, joined the ELLE Decor A-List firm in 2008 and became a partner in 2019. Together the duo give grand rooms an ease and wit that keep formality in check.

Houser considers “interior designer” an insufficient description for Redd and Kaihoi. “I feel like they are set curators,” she says. She gave them considerable freedom, occasionally inviting them to surprise her with potential purchases. “I don’t think it’s my place to hinder creative expression, and, as a result, the rooms are just magical.”

The house’s existing architecture gave that freedom some useful structure. “It had great English bones,” Redd says. He retained the original living room mantelpiece, library doors, and front door, while upstairs he and Kaihoi worked to restore character lost to earlier alterations that had stripped away 19th-century details. An excavation created additional basement space for a screening room and a ping-pong area, giving the family more places to gather and play.

From first visit to completion, the project took the better part of an unhurried decade, with pauses for permits, the Covid pandemic, and other commitments along the way. All the while Redd and Kaihoi kept shopping at auctions, antiques stores, and fairs, which ensured that the rooms would have an accumulated character. The designers looked to the guards at Buckingham Palace for the entrance hall and main staircase, borrowing the red and gold of their uniforms for its matka silk walls trimmed with gold braiding. Beyond the hall, the green velvet–lined library opens into a living room papered to resemble a coromandel screen.

Upstairs, the primary bedroom and a sitting area occupy a long, narrow space. Green felt screen dividers give the bed its own area in one corner without a wall breaking up the suite. “It’s all sort of one big bedsit, which is a very English notion,” Redd says. A small painting of a parrot on one of the screens keeps Houser company at her bedside.

The couple wanted their suite to feel like a luxurious hotel—inviting places to read and work, a comfortable bed, and a generously scaled bathroom—without sacrificing a sense of home. “You’ve ruined hotels for us,” Redd recalls Houser telling him of their previous work.

Step Inside the Ultimate Christmas Retreat

But Houser’s favorite rooms are the ones that bring everyone together: the kitchen and the adjoining striped family room, which open onto the garden. It’s here where everyone gathers for meals and to relax. The connected spaces provide separate places to eat, play games, and watch television, allowing the family to spend time together even when everyone is doing something different.

“Their design helps bring us together,” Houser says. “And that’s what it is. It’s a family home.”

#6831. These Cashmere Perfumes Are the Equivalent of Quiet Luxury in a Bottle

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Like wrapping yourself in a $400 cashmere sweater.

Not much compares to the snuggly feeling of a cashmere sweater against your skin, especially in cold weather. But if you don’t want to drop the $$$ on a whole cashmere wardrobe (if you do, make sure it’s Quince’s affordable cashmere), the next-best thing is a cashmere perfume.

Obviously, cashmere itself doesn’t have a smell. It’s a fabric, after all! But perfumers have found a way to capture that fluffy, cozy feeling through an accord called “cashmeran”—a blend of fragrance notes designed to evoke the soft, warm sensation of cashmere. Usually, that means it’s aldehydic (like fresh linens and cotton), warm, and a little bit powdery. But perfumes can still fall into the “cashmere” bucket even without a specific accord, usually using notes like creamy sandalwood and vanilla, warm amber and tonka bean, and even cloud-like musk and jasmine.

Smelling like cashmere has been my preferred way to embrace the homey feeling heading into winter. Nestling beside a fire, drinking a hot beverage, and wearing—you guessed it—a cashmere sweater has never been easier, so long as I have one of the below scents sprayed on. Keep reading for Cosmo editors’ top seven picks from brands like Byredo, Orebella, Phlur, and NOYZ.

"I always thought powdery perfumes would make me smell like a grandma, but either I'm inching toward grandma status or Angel Dust by Fugazzi is built different. It's powdery but not from heavy florals. Instead, the scent combines a mix of skin-like cashmere, musk, and amber. It's soft and reminiscent of the inside of a knitwear shop.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

“It’s hard to find a fragrance that straddles freshness and coziness, but this one does exactly that. Its moniker comes from a Brazilian Portuguese expression that describes the sensation of good vibes, happiness, and optimism, and it’s fair to say this perfume captures that sentiment perfectly. It oozes Brazilian warmth and sunniness and makes me want to book a trip to Rio.”—CELIA SHATZMAN, CONTRIBUTING WRITER

“I rely on body mists immediately after I get out of the shower to lightly fragrance my skin without feeling like too much. This one is my go-to right now (and the inspiration behind my current cashmere fragrance obsession). Even with nothing on (spicy!), my skin smells so toasty. It makes me want to immediately nuzzle into a plush robe.”—BG

“Unmute has a little bit of everything—juiciness from the plum note, spice from the cinnamon, musk from the amber, warmth from the sage, and sweetness from the pistachio—all wrapped in a cozy, comforting vanilla base. There’s something sexy and self-assured about the scent, without feeling too put-on or artificial.”—DANIELLE HARDMAN, CONTRIBUTING WRITER

“Milky perfumes are my favorite category of fragrance lately, and this does the lactonic, creamy fragrance so well. It’s not too sweet or cloying; instead, it leans on resinous amber, oak, and tonka bean to balance the scent. It’s like sitting by a fire, wrapped in the softest knits.”—BG

"I usually associate floral perfumes with spring and summer, but this one is undeniably perfect for cooler weather. Odéon is a smooth, rich rose scent, with a hefty dose of amber and cashmeran that gives it depth. It's inspired by Parisian cafe culture—and since I studied abroad there (hold the eyerolls), I feel qualified to say that this scent embodies the city’s romantic allure. It smells like window shopping in boutiques I can't afford and moonlit strolls along the Seine."—LAUREN GRUBER, COMMERCE EDITOR

“It’s an icon for a reason! Cashmere Mist is the OG of cashmere scents—it’s musky, powdery, and creamy, with soft woods and airy florals. My aunt was obsessed with this perfume in my youth, and I remember sneaking sprays of it whenever I wanted to feel like a ~sophisticated lady~”—BG
