#6804. The Sisters Behind Dannijo Are Making Sports Merch Cool

DannijoPro was built on the idea that female fans deserve game-day gear they actually want to wear.

When Knicks frenzy overtook New York City this past summer during the team’s historic championship run, women fans—both long-suffering devotees and bandwagoners alike—went looking for something to wear. And in a city known for its fashion sense and individuality, the same old generic logo shirts and jerseys simply would not do.

DannijoPro answered the call. Seemingly overnight, the brand’s cropped blue satin bombers, bedazzled denim jackets with crochet collars, and artfully embroidered vintage sports tees took over Instagram, with celebrities like Brooke Shields, cool girls, and Ali Brunson, wife of star point guard Jalen Brunson, and Jordyn Woods, who wed center Karl-Anthony Towns shortly after he won the chip, photographed wearing DannijoPro. Knicks royalty Spike Lee even wore a DannijoPro custom jacket courtside at Game 5.

Danielle and Jodie (Danni + Jo, get it?) Snyder, the sisters and co-founders behind the bespoke fanwear company (a sporty spinoff of their original jewelry and fashion brand, Dannijo), say they were just happy to be along for the historic ride. “No one could have predicted the Knicks were going to go as far as they did, but that was amazing for us,” Jodie says of the win that ended a 53-year title drought. “People loved what we were doing.” Their Knicks finals shirts sold out three times; all 800 satin bombers (priced at $395) sold out, too. Overall, the sisters tell me their sales for 2026 are up 200 percent from last year. “I’ve never seen anything like it in my life,” says Danielle, who, along with Jodie, lived in NYC for over a decade and visited during the finals. “The energy was electric and there was such community. People were buying merch because it was so much more than a garment. It’s a piece of history. It’s a relic now.”

The Knicks run may have fueled their virality, but DannijoPro’s popularity also stems from the fact that it is serving an audience that has been ignored for too long by companies that thought that, when designing sports merch for women, it was enough to “pink it and shrink it.” And although 75 percent of women identify as fans of one or more sports, and women’s sports merch is estimated to be a $4 billion market, still a quarter of female fans say they can’t find styles they like. “It’s crazy that we’re in 2026 and you can go to a game and not find anything you want to buy as a female fan,” Jodie says.

Three months after the Knicks took home the trophy, I reach the sisters as they are gearing up for the launch of their new NBA collection for the 2026-27 season and a number of other new partnerships that have come in the wake of their viral turn, including one rolling out this month with their hometown team, the Jacksonville Jaguars—DannijoPro’s first NFL collab. Danielle is in the brand’s atelier in Tiburon in Marin County, California; as we chat on Zoom, I see people hand-stitching various garments in the background. Jodie joins the call from her car in Jacksonville, Florida, where she heads up the company’s retail and business strategy.

The sisters, who are three years apart, grew up as close as could be in Jacksonville. They’ve been creative, crafty, and entrepreneurial from a young age. Their mom, a mental health counselor, didn’t have pierced ears, but she did have a killer clip-on earring collection, and the girls were obsessed with it. In middle school, Jodie took an interest in making jewelry of her own, and Danielle, wanting to do everything her big sister did, followed right along.

Their dad is a cardiologist, and they taught themselves how to do wire work using his medical tools. Soon, they were collaborating on intricately beaded designs and selling them in local stores. “We’d see kids around school wearing our handmade designs and they didn’t even know it was us making them,” Danielle says. “What we were doing was really fresh and cool. Girls and women could take our accessories and update a white tee and jeans, or anything basic, and make it their own. That was our first taste of using style to individualize and to tell stories and we caught the bug.”

They got their big break a few years later when Danielle was in middle school. Neiman Marcus bought 150 of their turquoise nugget necklaces. “I remember staying up until 3 or 4 in the morning cranking them out,” Danielle says. “And I had an exam the next morning.” The Snyders continued to balance school and their design ambitions. Years later, when Jodie was at the University of Florida studying business and public relations and Danielle at Vanderbilt, where she majored in psychology and Spanish, Jodie would fly up and they would do trunk shows at sororities on campus. “We were always making stuff for wherever we were in life,” Jodie explains. “And everything we do is rooted in sisterhood and wanting to be together, wanting to express ourselves,” Danielle adds.

Jodie graduated from college in 2004 and moved to New York City, with Danielle following in 2007. Dannijo was founded the following year and, like the Snyders’ childhood company, quickly became known for its statement jewelry pieces. Their collection was sold in Bergdorf Goodman and Henri Bendel; characters on Gossip Girl and anchors on the Today show wore their necklaces; Beyoncé and Kim Kardashian did too (purchasing them themselves). “I remember the saleswoman calling us and saying, ‘You’re never going to believe who bought the Ruth Celia necklace,’” Danielle says. It was Queen B, who went on to wear it at Coachella, courtside at NBA games, and on a trip with her husband, Jay-Z, in Europe. “So we started getting press before we even really knew what PR was.”

They soon expanded into fashion, designing the “perfect silk slip dress” modeled after Kate Moss in the ’90s. When Jodie’s daughter was 3, she went to a tie-dye party and the next thing they knew, Dannijo was selling tie-dyed hoodies. They styled it over a slip dress and it went viral. “We sold thousands of tie-dye hoodies—it was insane,” Jodie says. “Basically, we just made things that we wanted to wear.” Collabs with Starbucks, Mercedes-Benz, and Swarovski followed.

In 2020, as the pandemic took hold in NYC, the sisters, like many residents, decamped for other parts of the country. Jodie moved back to Florida, while Danielle moved to the Bay Area. The move across the country was hard on Danielle, who was suffering from postpartum depression. “I was lost, lonely, and I didn’t have community,” she notes.

One day, her husband came home with Golden State tickets and told her they were going out. “That first Warriors game changed my life,” Danielle explains. “It changed my perspective. It got me excited again—I was excited to root for a team and to be a part of something.” They started attending games regularly and Danielle began creating her own designs to wear. “I started going through my husband’s closet and cutting up all of his old jerseys and making them into outfits for me,” she says. “My fix is always to be creative, so I was using my hands, doing stitch work, adding crystals, what have you.” Other female fans at the games began taking notice of her creative designs and asking where she had bought them. “Everyone was noticing because there was just nothing like it out there, so I called Jodie and I said, ‘This is really big.’” Soon, Dannijo’s sporty sister brand, DannijoPro, was born.

Launching a company focused on women fans was a “no-brainer,” Danielle says. “When I started making myself stuff to wear, that’s when I noticed what a crazy white space it was. Sports have never been more popular, but the product just had not caught up to that reality.” It took a year and a half to get an NBA license, but once they did, the first DannijoPro collection, which rolled out in 2024 at the Chase Center (where the Warriors play), quickly sold out.

As ever, the sisters are their own inspiration. Jodie has two children and Danielle has three. “Our worlds have changed,” Jodie says. “We’re both in T-shirts and sweatshirts; we’re not getting dressed up every day anymore.” As such, the DannijoPro items they make aren’t just for game day. They are for the 70 percent of women fans who say they’ve bought sports merch, while only 51 percent have bought tickets to attend an event. “We wanted to create items that people want to wear on and off the court or field,” Jodie says. “It's about making flattering silhouettes and clothes we all want to wear, and then applying them to the teams that we’re excited about.”

DannijoPro has three main products: officially licensed merch complete with the signature touches the designers have come to be known for, like crochet collars, hand-stitching, painting, and crystal work; “blanks” that can be customized with the help of its in-house artists (most orders come via DM); and vintage pieces that are reworked by hand.

As Jodie talks, Danielle runs around the atelier pulling out products from their new collections to show me. In August, they launched designs they made for avatars in the NBA 2K game. In September, they debuted a collab with Roger Federer’s Laver Cup tennis tournament. Their new NBA collection, called “Game Day 2.0,” rolled out last month, while their Jacksonville Jaguars line will go on sale on November 17. Future partnerships with other teams and leagues are in the works.

The sisters see their new company as “so much bigger than a fashion brand,” Danielle says. “We are reimagining fandom through a culture lens, and it’s about people. We’re living in a world of AI where everything’s like a bot. For us, it’s like, how do we bring really cool dynamic experiences in real life to people and create connections and community?”

“We think women fans should be celebrated in that there are products out there that speak to us,” Jodie adds. “It’s more than merch. I think it stands for so many other things. We can’t just accept that it’s okay to just slap logos on things, or pink it and shrink it, and women are just going to go with that. And at the end of the day, all these companies forget, but women have the buying power.”

#6802. The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

#6799. Mark Ballas Responds to Speculation He Might Become a Judge on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

Mark Ballas is one of the most talented pros on Dancing With the Stars, but fans have been speculating that he could pivot to being a judge on the series after getting a taste of judging life over on Dancing With the Stars: The Next Pro.

So...does Mark plan to hang up his dancing shoes and join the judges’ panel? He told The Hollywood Reporter in a recent interview that the verdict is still out. “I don’t know. I’m not sure. I’d have to see,” he said, somewhat cryptically. “I’m a very take it day-by-day person. Honestly, I don’t think ahead like that. I’d have to see if it came along. I enjoyed guest judging. The Next Pro was really fun. I think I’ve done the show for long enough that I could give good feedback and advice for contestants. But, again, that’s totally out of my hands.”

He also noted elsewhere in the interview that “I’ve only guest judged once. I love the creative part of the show. That’s what makes it fun. That’s kind of been my life for so long. And one of the big reasons for me going back last season was excitement for my son to see me do what I’ve dedicated my life to. He’s only three, but he can watch the show and he’s like, ‘Oh my God, dada!’ So it’s a big part of it, and constantly loving the new challenges and creativity. It’s pretty straightforward, simple as that, really.”

Oh, AND? Mark was also asked about his mom, Shirley Ballas, potentially joining the Dancing With the Stars judges’ panel full-time, and teased “I have no idea. She’s already a judge on Strictly. That would be difficult to do both, but Len [Goodman] did it for so long. So did Bruno [Tonioli]. I have no idea what the intentions are with the panel. I think the panel is pretty set.”

#6798. Misha & Puff’s First Womenswear Collection Gives Knitwear a New Point of View

The cult-favorite children’s knitwear brand is officially expanding to adults.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Women’s fashion has some of the best designs out there—full stop. That said, every now and then I see a well-dressed kid and think, “Damn, I wish I had something like that.” This is especially true when I see a little one sporting a design from none other than Misha & Puff, a brand known for its ethically crafted, handmade, vintage-inspired children’s clothes. And while my wardrobe carries a playful touch, I’m not necessarily looking for a supersized version of a child’s sweater, but rather the essence of that whimsy. Thankfully, I don’t have to look too far; Misha & Puff just launched its first-ever women’s collection—and it’s more than I could have hoped for.

“Knits are hot. Knits are sexy. The way that they drape, the way that they accentuate your neck, I really do feel like we can do a lot with a knit,” Sophia Roe told ELLE.com over the phone. “For me, I feel really beautiful when I put these clothes on.” The James Beard Award-winning chef—along with her daughter, Mizu—came together with artist Mara Hoffman behind the lens to create a campaign that captures the essence of Misha & Puff, highlighting the craftsmanship and expression of the brand’s new womenswear collection.

“Everything I do with the brand is very authentic and really about what I’m interested in...That’s how the kids’ brand started, with me having my son and then growing up,” Misha & Puff’s founder, Anna Wallack, said on a phone call. “The choice to launch women’s in a bigger way really comes from the pull that I’m feeling as a woman. For me, dressing women and dressing myself is about how we’re working these pieces together. How do I feel in these silhouettes? Who is this character today? I want to keep playing with that in a bigger way.”

Wallack went on to tell me that designing for the collection—which launched today—started with what she described as a “fiber-first” approach. “We work with a Pima cotton in Peru that worked so well for kids because it’s incredibly soft and it’s so wearable, and then to find that I want to wear that all the time for myself; I want to wear our merino wool,” she explained. “Our decision to launch women’s with a bigger collection really was a commitment to doing it instead of just making one sweater here and there.” The result? A range of expressive knits in a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s that encourage the wearer to be who she is as a woman today. With every solid- or heather-hued piece there’s another featuring an eye-catching print or embroidery detail. “I feel a little more permission to explore other ideas in the adult collection and what it means to be a women, too.”

This exploration extends into Misha & Puff’s campaign. Roe shared on a phone call that the idea of partnering with Misha & Puff stemmed from just being a fan of the brand. “I said, ‘I don’t know what we can do but I really want to do something with Anna.’ Little did I know that in the background she was working on this unbelievable adult collection,” she said. Though this isn’t the first project Roe has starred in with her daughter, she explained it was the first where she was shot by another mother, making the experience feel truly special—especially as she comes into her body 15 months postpartum. “I feel kind of hot as a mom. There are a lot of narratives out there about getting back to whatever that pre-baby thing is, and I just kind of rebuke that in every single way. I think what I am now is kind of amazing and I really do feel like Mara was able to bring that out in a way that no one else could have.”

On the vision for the campaign, Hoffman shared that the focus was “seeing how does a woman exist through a space or on the street, and how does she live in it?” Working to capture Roe in a documentarian way, the artist wanted to make sure the imagery felt authentic to both the brand and the people wearing it. “We are lucky to be able to capture little shots of that along the way.” Of course, shooting with a baby can come with its own unpredictability. Luckily, as Roe shared, working with someone like Hoffman helped her relax on set with the reminder, “This is just life.”

An astrophysicist, a barrel racer, and a funeral director walk into a castle.…

#6791. Understated, Earthy, and Endlessly Wearable: The Best Olive Green Polishes

The earthy shade adds an edge to fall manicures while still wearing like a neutral.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like all nail polish colors, green has a vast spectrum, one that runs from electric neon to bold kelly to soft sage and all the way down to deep, moody olive. Of all the greens out there, olive sits in a quieter place. The hue is earthy and a little mysterious, somewhere between a vintage army jacket and an actual juicy olive. It’s a shade we love for fall, since it pairs so seamlessly with black, rich browns, burgundy, red, and, of course, cream.

Once reserved for the brave few, olive has now gone mainstream. Part of the appeal is how wearable it is, offering an unexpected twist without ever feeling too loud. It’s also soft and soothing to the eye, making it an easy shade to wear day after day.

If you’re new to the shade, the easiest way in is to treat it like a neutral. Try it with a camel coat and chunky knits when the weather turns, or a crisp white shirt for something fresh and unexpected. A creamy, opaque finish feels polished and modern, while a glossy jelly or a hint of shimmer adds depth in low light. However you wear it, olive looks effortlessly considered and adds an edge to your style.

Below, we’ve rounded up the best olive green nail polishes to shop now.

A personal favorite, Elia is a muted olive green that looks rich from the very first stroke. The opaque formula glides on evenly in a single swipe and dries to a soft gloss that lasts through daily wear.

Manucurist’s take on olive green feels a touch brighter. It sits beautifully on light and dark skin tones alike and delivers an opaque wash of color. Part of the “Green Flash” collection, it cures under an LED gel lamp and lasts for nearly 21 days.

Essie’s Totally Plaid is an olive green polish with a cream finish that delivers a gel-like shine and depth while still removing as easily as a regular polish. The formula applies smoothly in two coats, giving you rich, opaque color without the need for a UV lamp.

This deep and moody olive green shade is the epitome of effortless chic, pairing easily with everything from neutral knits to bold jewel tones.

Inspired by the lush landscapes of the Mediterranean, this olive green evokes a lighter take on the olive color spectrum. It’s a tad brighter and slightly more yellow-toned, making it a great pick if you want a fresh, sunlit green this fall.

O.P.I’s Suzi is the chic olive green and wardrobe staple of the olive family. It’s a bit darker-toned, radiating earthy warmth and understated elegance. Plus, it’s fast-drying and offers up to seven days of wear.

For those seeking a little more sparkle, this olive green jelly is packed with large green holographic glitters that create a textured finish and catch the light with every movement, adding a spark of joy to your manicure.

Calming and serene, this moss green shade is part of the “Campground Collection”, a four-shade assortment of earthy, comforting colors. Its buttery-smooth application matches the shade’s peaceful look.

This may not look like your average olive green at first glance, but that’s because it has taupe undertones. Once it’s on your nails, you’ll notice the hint of green was there all along. The result is a soft, grounded neutral that pairs effortlessly with cozy fall knits and earthy accessories.

This nail color may be named Pickle, but we see the olive in there. This tangy green with muted yellow undertones feels lively while remaining neutral enough to pair with everything in your wardrobe.

A true olive green, Extra Dirty brings rich, earthy depth to your nails. All you need is one to two coats for a creamy manicure worthy of a fall-season spotlight.

#6790. From “Magnetic Merlot” to “Soft Licorice,” 10 Dark, Minimalist Manicure Ideas to Try This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Bolder hues are getting the starring role as winter sets in

While soap manicures, subtle petal nails, and longevity techniques once dominated the summer months, a darker color palette is quickly taking over now that the clocks have gone back and evenings are early. Yet if you're hesitant to venture into "too bold" territory or fear a pop of color on your fingertips, a dark manicure doesn't have to feel overly attention-grabbing.

In fact, as professional nail technician Samantha Rudge shares, dark manicures with a more minimalist finish are proving to be winter's most popular manicure trend. "For this season, I think we'll be seeing darker classic manicures with minimalist touches," she shares, adding that she has already seen an uptick in shade requests for Bio Sculpture's rich, chocolate-toned Decadent Cocoa and the ever-popular Garnet Noir, a moody red-black hue. "Nail designs like chrome, cat eye, micro French tips, and tortoiseshell will also dominate this season," she adds.

"Short nails are also very popular right now, no matter the nail art or shades," she adds. Regardless of shape or color, Rudge stresses the importance of prioritizing nail health at this time of year. To protect nail longevity and prevent chipping, Rudge recommends assessing your natural nail condition, choosing the right base (BIAB or gel), and using a nourishing cuticle oil to repair and strengthen brittle nails.

Ahead, discover 10 new dark manicures for those who love a more minimalist finish, from “Garnet Noir” nails to subtle lines.

Somewhere between a square and an oval, these squoval nails look sophisticated and smart—plus, paired with the deep red “garnet noir” shade, they make a strong case for opting for a shorter length.

The epitome of minimalist nail art, add subtle intrigue to your manicure this winter via a curved line created on the cuticles. Choose complementary shades to recreate it: think cherry red with hot pink; navy blue and khaki green.

Taking over the SS27 runways this season, purple is finally having a moment in the spotlight. When it comes to nail colors, look for richer hues with a jewel-tone finish to keep the manicure elevated and modern. Rudge recommends Bio Sculpture's Erica shade.

All shades of wine are a staple as the festive season approaches, but why not add a chrome effect for extra sparkle?

Coffee tones are becoming one of the most popular color stories in nail salons this winter. These teeny-tiny dots make for a unique switch-up and can be created using a micro dotting tool.

A subtle marble effect elevates an otherwise classic burgundy manicure.

While almond and square are among the most popular of nail shapes, don't underestimate a soft oval. Paired with a classic cabernet tone, this dark manicure strikes the balance of looking impactful yet soft.

If you're taking a break from acrylics or longer lengths, a super-short licorice manicure is the way to do it. Add a glossy top coat and keep the nails short and square.

In the mood to experiment? Simply switch up each nail with chocolate swirls, block colors, or a French tip to recreate this look.

This ultra-glossy midnight blue manicure is all about a high-shine, mirrorlike finish that could be created with a chrome powder or top coat.

#6789. This Puget Sound Home Kept All of Its Original Charm—While Hiding Every Modern Convenience

The design team faithfully restored the details that have the most heart.

When this Puget Sound home was built in 1908, its original owners gave it a name: Heart’s Delight. Years later, after the house was bought by another family, principal designer Lisa Staton, alongside her senior designer Lauren Galeotti, took on the task of restoring the space with that same sentiment in mind.

“My favorite thing about this house is all of the love it holds,” Galeotti says. The new owners became deeply attached to the space, and the neighbors had long adored the house, too. As work progressed, the contractors and tradespeople fell for it as well. “She really charmed us all, from start to finish,” Galeotti says of the home.

That affection guided a renovation rooted in respect for what was already there. “Our passion for heritage homes is fueled by the original craftsmanship, history, and charm the homes hold,” Staton explains.

Every piece of original millwork stayed, and any new elements added had to take their cues from the old—even if that meant undoing some of the design missteps of the past. For example, a custom, made-to-match fireplace now sits over the original brick-and-plaster hearth that had been painted white. Though it was a whole team effort to get right, it now flawlessly complements the living room’s woodwork.

The current homeowners are also avid entertainers, so the kitchen needed a total strategic (and stylistic) rework that wouldn’t expand beyond its footprint. Adding the backsplash tile, created with Dorset Delft artist Aviva Halter, and a textural floor tile were key to giving the room its inimitable character. Other splurges, like all-new lighting throughout and the powder room's striking mural wallpaper, were necessary investments in the home's future.

But the biggest one, Staton says, was restoring all original details that everyone had fallen in love with: "always worth the investment!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: Lisa Staton and Lauren Galeotti of Lisa Staton Interior DesignStylist: Cozbi Jean Location: Puget Sound, WashingtonThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-bathroom Craftsman-style home built in 1908

This living room already “glows with daylight” thanks to its original windows, and the millwork has charm and texture in spades, so the main task in updating this room was to create a cozy, after-hours vibe.

“We wanted to make it warm and inviting, and every surface and corner needed to be enveloping,” Staton says. She achieved this by mixing newly sourced finds with well-loved pieces from the family’s personal collection, and it all blends seamlessly.

After entering the home through the original French doors, guests are greeted by the great room. This entertaining-ready space is faithful to its original style with two very notable additions: the custom fireplace cladding and accordion panels directly above that open to reveal the TV. It’s a room that can easily turn a quick drop-by into an entire evening in. “A pair of matching sofas invite you to sit and chat for a while,” Staton adds.

Keeping the spirit of this kitchen intact while gut-renovating it was a challenge, but the trick was to hide away the modern conveniences wherever possible and draw the eye toward period-appropriate accents instead.

“In heritage homes, it is really helpful to bring in antique elements to the space to add warmth and character in imperfect items,” Galeotti says. In this case, the flush mount ceiling lights, stools, window treatments, and hutch bring in texture and patina that add to the room’s authenticity.

Combining the storage and function of a kitchen island with the rustic character of a cook’s table, the kitchen’s central surface feels more like a piece of furniture than an extension of the cabinetry. “Since we did not need to put any appliances in the island, that really became an opportunity to keep beauty at the forefront,” Galeotti explains.

One of the team’s favorite parts of the kitchen, though, lies in the smaller details: the handmade backsplash tiles, which bring a personal, playful touch. “The client chose the subject matter of each custom-painted tile, including meaningful flowers and even their pets!” Galeotti says. Their Dorset Delft style makes the stoneware feel like it has always belonged in the house.

Though space is certainly tight in this pantry, it’s a workhorse area in its own right. “We designed custom cabinetry to line the walls with plenty of storage, and were even able to squeeze in a wall oven, microwave, and warming drawer,” Galeotti notes. This discreet appliance placement also helps the main cook space retain its ageless atmosphere.

The homeowners asked for this newly added powder room to feel “whimsical,” and Staton’s team granted that wish, selecting a dusky Andrew Martin mural wallcovering that depicts a landscape with traditional folk art motifs. Its towering pear trees, mythical creatures, and rambling vales read like an epic fairy tale across the four walls. “It is a small room, but we wanted to make a big impact,” she says.

According to Galeotti, “the dining room lives in the same footprint and largely looks exactly like the day it was built!” The team restored the original built-in cabinet, whose diamond-patterned panes echo the room’s leaded-glass windows. This clean-line geometry contrasts beautifully with the curves, plumes, and ruffled silhouettes seen in the window treatments and Murano glass chandelier.

Updating the primary bedroom’s layout was another necessary correction to previous design choices—the team took down a wall and turned two rooms into one. “However, given how the windows are on the large wall where the rooms used to be divided, it may have originally been one space,” Galeotti adds. Now, there’s an unobstructed view out to the water and mountains just beyond the property. They also added a his-and-hers closet, new flooring, and a bed Galeotti describes as a “perfect cloud” from which to greet the surrounding scenery each day.

Previously, the primary bathroom was completely open to the bedroom, with carpeting and a step up to access it. “It was a bit of a strange situation,” Staton admits. A serious layout rework was in order so that the space is now separate, level with the bedroom, and equipped with a freestanding tub and beautifully tiled shower. “We also added a feature we do in most bathrooms: use vintage or antique mirrors as doors to create medicine cabinets,” she adds.

“The addition of this bedroom at the back of the house gives guests a really private retreat in a quiet corner,” Galeotti says. With its bobbin bed frame, antique furniture finds, and misty color palette, this sleep space provides a soft place to land.

“This is the kids’ bathroom, but we imagined a bathroom that felt beautiful for children, guests, and grown kids alike,” Galeotti notes. The space originally suffered from the same “watch the step” quirk that the main bathroom did, so Staton installed a similar solution here: She put the plumbing into the floor and incorporated all-new finishes and fixtures to add elegant polish that all ages can appreciate.

Lisa Staton Interior Design is a boutique firm established in 2004 with roots in the East Coast and Seattle, united by a shared love for enhancing older homes and creating new ones inspired by historic craftsmanship. The team brings a unique blend of both modern and traditional elements to a wide array of homes.

#6784. There’s Major New Evidence That Robert Pattinson Is the Next James Bond

Hardly a day goes by without a report dropping that someone’s cousin’s friend’s brother overheard someone else’s cousin’s friend’s brother at a bar say that [insert hot British actor here] is the next James Bond. And so far, Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve have not confirmed one. single. thing.

However, we do know that several actors screen-tested for the iconic role, including Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor, and—surprise!—Robert Pattinson. And according to Matthew Belloni in Puck News, there’s reason to think Robert is favored to become the next 007.

Turns out all potential Bonds sign contracts for the film and its follow-ups—and three separate sources told Belloni that while Jack and Josh “were required to sign up for four Bonds,” Robert “was able to lower that commitment to three” and negotiate himself “a whole lot more money than the others.”

So, what does that mean? Yet more sources explain that since Robert’s “test deal was so difficult to close,” people close to the situation are “wondering why the filmmakers would bother if they didn’t really want him.” Innnnnteresting!

There’s definitely some discourse about whether or not Bond should be an established household name (traditionally, he isn’t), with former casting director Debbie McWilliams—who cast the last three Bonds—telling The Independent, “It is absolutely essential that he retains a total enigma. I don’t want to see any of them as Bond because we now know so much about them. We want to know as little about them personally as possible, because that what spies are. We don’t need to know where he goes shopping or who his parents are, or where he lives. We never want to see him at home. And a vital element of the whole thing is his job description. He’s licensed to kill, and we have to believe that he can do that. If you don’t, then you’ve lost the audience.”

Casting Robert Pattinson definitely challenges that approach, but we’ll see!

#6783. Zimmermann Hosts a Star-Studded Soirée in Downtown New York City

Katie Holmes, Uma Thurman, and Naomi Watts came out to celebrate the brand’s cruise 2027 collection.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Inside Indochine’s red-lit, retro-inspired French-Vietnamese dining room in New York City, Zimmermann gathered friends of the brand to celebrate its cruise 2027 collection. Presented on the sun-drenched Côte d’Azur in June, the label’s latest served as the perfect bit of vacation and occasion-ready dressing for November in New York. Now that the lineup has officially hit stores, fashion insiders and fans of the brand alike (including Uma Thurman, Eve Hewson, Katie Holmes, and Naomi Watts) showed up dripping in ruffled gowns, sailor-inspired stripes, chunky shearlings, and ethereal lace and silks from the collection. Against the dark, 1940s-inspired tropical salon interior of Indochine, it was the ideal mix of downtown energy and New York glamour.

As champagne flowed through the bar area, glasses clinked while miniature crab cakes and spring rolls were passed. Zimmermann’s sister duo Nicky and Simone Zimmermann could be spotted catching up with friends new and old. “We have a very, very close connection with New York,” Nicky told ELLE in between greeting guests. “It was pretty much when I started the business, one of the first places internationally that I went to in the ’90s, and it blew my mind. It’s been inspirational to us 100 percent.” It was a rare occasion for the brand; for years, Zimmermann has shown at Paris Fashion Week (after presenting in New York in the mid-2010s) and the duo oscillates between Sydney and Paris for most of the year.

Zimmermann’s painterly and intrinsically bohemian flourishes could be seen in the many outfits of guests, all highlighting the vivid cruise collection. Think: decadent silky draped drop-waisted gowns and paneled lace maxi dresses, or, for the more demure, sleek all-black looks that captured the New York essence. “For me, there’s a little bit of a sense of humor behind it—without insulting anybody—it was about the story when Australia actually beat America in the America’s Cup, which is a very famous sailing race,” Nicky told ELLE of the collection’s inspiration.

“I think it was in 1983, and America had not lost the America’s Cup in something like 173 years; it was a ridiculous winning streak, and Australia came along. The inspiration is more about a David and Goliath kind of story,” she continued. “We came along as the Aussies, not established in the sailing world. They were very cheeky, rogue, kind of Aussie young guys up against this amazing, sophisticated establishment of the New York Yacht Club. And they never lost—and we beat them. So in Australia, it’s a legendary story.” Nicky combined the sportiness of sailing gear with the sort of elite New York Yacht Club preppiness, resulting in colorful silk scarf embellishment or sweaters decked out in accessorized pins. “They would hold the most incredible parties and the women were so glamorous and sophisticated and beautiful and not anything that Australia could relate to at the time, so I loved the contrast.”

Conversation was flowing and blooming anthuriums covered the tables as guests took their seats. Nicky kicked off the dinner with a speech, musing on her early days in New York on the Lower East Side and the many parties she attended in Tribeca before sharing more inspiration behind the collection: “It’s about establishment, and then it’s about people, you know, working really hard, coming together as a team, defeating the undefeatable,” she said, with a laugh.

Next came an intimate performance by the singer and Bad Monkey actress Charlotte Lawrence, who sang two of her original songs, then led a folksy sing-along to “Dreams” by Fleetwood Mac. Even the most guarded of celebs and fashion’s elite couldn’t help but bob their heads and sing a tune. As guests dined on a family-style mix of kale salad, spicy shrimp, grilled salmon, barbecued chicken, and steak, the champagne glass clinks slowed as candles slowly flickered and the evening closed out with lemon tarts.

A little yacht-club polish, a little Aussie irreverence: Zimmermann celebrated cruise 2027 in high style.

#6781. The 10 Best Hair Wavers Bazaar Editors Are Actually Using

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Get beach hair all year round with these top-rated tools

As someone with pin-straight Asian hair, I have always admired waves and curls. I have slick, straight, stubborn hair, and—most frustratingly—it resists holding a curl. I’ve tried every trick in the book: extra-hold mousses, setting my curls with pins, and the best strong-hold hairsprays. Nothing seemed to do the trick—until I discovered hair wavers, specialized curling irons that create bends instead of twists.

In recent years, hair wavers have grown in popularity because they're easy to use and deliver effortless-looking results. “The effect [of a waver] is less curled and more textured. It’s great for that effortless, tousled vibe or adding body to finger hair without that much bounce,” says celebrity hairstylist Danielle Priano. Ahead, Priano shares her picks for the best hair wavers, along with some of the Bazaar beauty team’s favorites.

Every time my hairstylist uses this waver at the salon, I notice that my waves can stay for up to three to four days, which is considered a long time for my hair type. The waves also look so natural that they give me that “just permed my hair” effect. Priano is a fan of the tool too, saying, “It heats evenly and creates long-lasting waves with a sleek finish.” Plus, it has tons of features like a two-hour auto shutoff and a clear digital display.

You may have seen this on social media, and for good reason: At under $50, it is a great option for budget-conscious users. It’s also highly rated for both long and short hair and is easy (and safe!) for beginners to use because it has no exposed barrel. We also love that it comes in 1- and 1.25-inch wave sizes, so you can choose the wand that best fits your length and style preference.

Priano also swears by Trademark Beauty’s double-barrel wave iron. “This one’s loved for its wider barrels and even heat distribution,” she says. “It’s great for longer hair or when you want that soft glam wave.” As a bonus, the tool also works well for shorter hair as it creates two waves rather than the usual three.

This is my go-to hair waver at the moment because it’s so versatile. You can adjust the barrel size to get the exact wave you want, from tight to soft and beachy. (And you also have full control over the temperature.) I’m super lazy, so I like to opt for something that has full capabilities in one.

You can’t beat this price, which makes Bed Head’s tool a great entry-level styler. That, along with its tourmaline ceramic barrels, 10 heat settings, and easy digital controls, makes it super straightforward to use. The barrels themselves are also on the large side, which creates a great beachy effect in the final look. And if you’re accident-prone, take comfort in the fact that it comes with a protective glove, too.

“This waver is pricey, at nearly $200, but it’s also the most versatile. It comes with three barrels—a waver, a rod, and a clamp—so you can create any type of wave or curl your heart desires,” says Bazaar beauty director Jenna Rosenstein. “I have thick hair, and it works just as well on my hair as it does on those with fine, thin hair, since the heat is customizable up to 450 degrees. It’s also great to travel with!”

This one is another Bazaar editor favorite: “While I do own two different hair wavers, they both serve a unique vibe. I love this INH Triple Barrel when I’m going for a looser, more lived-in wave,” says Sarah Olivieri, Bazaar senior designer. “It really gives the illusion of salty beach hair freshly dried from the sun.”

“I love the look of effortless waves, but creating seamless waves without creases or harsh demarcations between crimps can be tricky,” says Bazaar beauty market and commerce editor Katie Intner. “I love the jumbo Mane waver because the large barrel size gives a more natural wave and crimps hair in under a few minutes. Plus, it’s dual voltage, which is great for travel, and gives a surprising amount of wave longevity.” Even better, the base is detachable and can be used with any of the brand’s other hair tool attachments.

This waver is on the pricey side, but that’s because it has the brand’s next-gen features. Think: even heating and a curved—not cylindrical—shape on the barrels that gives that ideal “effortless” look. They’re also finished with an anodized coating that adds shine and reduces frizz. Who doesn’t want that?

If you aren’t a bona fide hairstyling expert and want a hair waver that doesn’t require much technique, this option from WavyTalk might be a good fit. It has a built-in timer you can set from four to 10 seconds that beeps when it’s time to move on to the next section of hair. Its deep V-shaped plates and dual-barrel design sculpt hair for a more pronounced wave.

Luckily, it’s “fairly straightforward,” Priano says. “You start by sectioning your hair; the smaller the section, the more defined the wave. Then, working from the roots down, clamp the waver onto the hair and hold for a few seconds, usually 5 to 10, depending on your hair type and heat setting. Move down the same strand, overlapping slightly to continue the wave pattern all the way to the ends. Repeat throughout the entire head, and once cooled, you can brush it out for a softer, more lived-in texture or leave it defined for more drama.”

What’s the Best Way to Prep Your Hair Before Using a Waver?

To minimize potential damage, you’ll want to do a few things first: “Always start with clean, dry hair; wavers are not meant for wet styling. I recommend applying a heat protectant from mid-lengths to ends to shield the hair from damage,” Priano adds. “If you’re looking to boost volume and hold, add a lightweight texturizing spray or a mousse before blow-drying. And don’t forget to detangle. Smooth, tangle-free strands help the waver glide better and create more uniform results.”

Is There a Difference Between a Waver and a Curling Iron?

“The main difference between a hair waver and a curling iron lies in the shape and result. A curling iron wraps hair around a single barrel, creating more classic, rounded curls or ringlets, depending on the size of the barrel and the technique,” Priano says. “A waver, on the other hand, presses the hair between barrels to form deep, wave-like ridges.”

#6780. Culture Watch: What to See and Where to Go in November

Culture Watch is a series spotlighting the intersection of fashion and art, bringing you the latest must-see exhibitions, pop-ups, installations, and more.

November is a good month to make a little room in your calendar. As the holiday season starts to gather pace, galleries, brands, and creative spaces are offering plenty of reasons to get out and look around. In New York, Salon Art + Design returns for its 15th year, while Loro Piana presents its “Master of Cashmere” installation at The Brant Foundation; farther afield, international exhibitions offer even more to explore. Check out all of this month’s best cultural happenings below.

Louis Vuitton Presents ‘La Valise Bruno Aveillan’ at LV Dream

For 13 years, photographer and filmmaker Bruno Aveillan has taken his Louis Vuitton Alzer suitcase to more than 100 destinations, making it a recurring presence in his work. Now, the well-traveled case is at the center of “La Valise Bruno Aveillan,” an immersive exhibition at LV Dream, the house’s encompassing destination in Paris. Aveillan’s photographs appear alongside historic Louis Vuitton trunks and a closer look at the craftsmanship behind the Alzer, the brand’s hard-sided suitcase built around a sturdy wooden frame. The exhibition marks the final chapter in the house’s yearlong celebration of the monogram’s 130th anniversary.

Loro Piana Names Its Cashmere of the Year and Unveils a ‘Master of Cashmere’ Installation

Loro Piana put cashmere in the spotlight at The Brant Foundation in New York, where it marked the 11th edition of its Cashmere of the Year Award. Established in 2015 and held stateside for the first time, the prize recognizes the herders who produce the finest cashmere fiber. Coinciding with the honor, which went to three couples of cashmere herders from Inner Mongolia, is “Master of Cashmere: The Quest for Fiber Excellence,” an installation that traces the material from its origins to the Maison’s work with it. The multi-room experience features archival sketches, explanatory timelines, and, of course, soft-to-the-touch cashmere.

Salon Art + Design Returns to the Park Avenue Armory for Its 15th Year

The 15th edition of Salon Art + Design is now on view at the Park Avenue Armory—and it’s one of its most immersive, expansive editions yet. As is tradition, the event brings together collectible design, fine art, and jewelry. The event is open through November 8. Standouts include Jean Dunand’s 1929 La Forêt lacquered screen, Michel Dufet’s 1918 daybeds, and Jean Royère’s illuminated desk. Fernando Mastrangelo’s totem lamps add a sculptural note, while the jewelry lineup includes pieces from Fernando Jorge, Mellerio, Cora Sheibani, and Viltier.

Lara Saget Debuts Her First New York Solo Show, ‘And the World Came Apart’

Lara Saget brings more than a decade of material experimentation to her first solo exhibition in New York. At Trotter&Sholer’s new Elizabeth Street space, her sculptures and wearable works fuse glass, stone, and metal through heat, pressure, and time. Drawing on geological processes and NASA data, the show moves from ancient natural formations to humanity’s rapid impact on the planet.

The Phillips Collection Opens ‘Avery, Gottlieb & Rothko: By the Sea’

Milton Avery went to Gloucester, Massachusetts, to “live like an artist,” then brought Adolph Gottlieb and Mark Rothko along for summers of creative exchange. In Washington, D.C., The Phillips Collection’s “Avery, Gottlieb & Rothko: By the Sea” brings together watercolors and color-drenched canvases, tracing what those Cape Ann days set in motion.

Tate St Ives Spotlights Wilhelmina Barns-Graham

What does a landscape feel like in the body? Scottish artist Wilhelmina Barns-Graham spent eight decades exploring that question through art shaped by sensation and memory. The retrospective at Tate St Ives in Cornwall, England, is the most comprehensive survey of her work, moving from early landscapes and studio interiors to later works alive with bold color and sweeping gestures.

Taylor Swift, take note: famously hard-to-get-into New York City hotspot The Corner Store has officially landed at The Cosmopolitan of Las Vegas, marking its first-ever location outside Manhattan. Expect its adventurous martinis, signature wood-paneled interiors by Rockwell Group, and a handful of Vegas-exclusive menu additions, including a Butcher’s Counter serving up wagyu tomahawk and porterhouse steaks.

#6777. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6775. A New Marni x Hoka Sneaker Is Here—Here’s What to Know

The Italian fashion house once again brings its unique touch to the athletic brand.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Last year, when Marni and Hoka launched their first collaboration, the two labels came together to bring the world a collection of puffy, chunky, candy-colored takes on the athletic footwear brand’s Bondi sneaker. Naturally, it was a hit among the streetwear set, including the likes of Steph Curry. Of course, a lot can change in the span of a year and a half—and the latest sneaker trends are no exception. In the same way jeans have gone from baggy styles to straight-leg and even skinny jeans, fashion has taken its sneakers from their dad eras to silhouettes that lean a little sleeker. The latest result of these turning tides: The Hoka Clifton Marni.

“Shifting from the maximalist and oversized aesthetic towards a calmer, more essential form made sense with Meryll [Rogge]’s ascension to Marni,” Thomas Cykana, Hoka’s senior director of global collaborations, tells ELLE in an email. “This affords a simple but surprising design that cues premium embellishments, metallic hardware, and unexpected prints over an overtly in-your-face design. This shift enables consumers to both craft their uniform and express their individuality without sacrificing Hoka’s pure performance capabilities.”

Available now, the design is the culmination of form and function. Marni brings its unique design perspective to the technical shoe, not only with the chosen colors—Orange, Black, and White—but also with design-forward touches such as woven heel tabs, windowpane-patterned insoles, and three shoelace options for each colorway.

“Marni has a unique ability to take everyday design cues and make them feel artful and unexpected. That lust for curiosity and imaginative nature is what first drew our team to the label and continues to inspire us,” Cykana shares. “Our debut collection in 2025 showed how much our teams share in creativity and craftsmanship, and that trust led us to our unique spin on the iconic Clifton. The editorial tone they delivered to this project reflects the perfect sensation of everyday and extraordinary at once.”

#6773. Here’s Everything We Know About ‘Campus Drivers’ Season 2 (and Which Couple It’ll Be About)

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Hello to everyone who watched all of Campus Drivers in one day and now urgently need to know what’s going on with season 2: We have answers about what comes next for Lois and Lane and their delightfully trope-y love story.

The first season of Campus Drivers saw Lois break up with her longtime boyfriend and have no choice but to move in with “campus bad boy” Lane in his ultra-fancy apartment. At first Lois can’t stand him, then they become fast friends, then there’s the whole “let’s make out in front of your ex to make him jealous” phase, and finally the season ends with Lane and Lois fully getting together...only for Lane’s mom to enroll him in a new school. Lois literally comes home to a bunch of movers and literally no information from her boyfriend, so yeah, obviously season 2 can’t come fast enough! Here’s what we know.

Not yet. Prime is likely in the “wait and see how it performs” stage of things, but the show is in their top 10, so a renewal seems pretty inevitable at this point.

Lane and Lois obviously have a lot of issues to resolve, but reminder: Campus Drivers is based on a book series. And if season 2 is based on book 2 (aka Crash Course), then a brand-new couple will take center stage: “college bad boy trying to change gears” Donovan and “romance-loving good girl” Carrie. Their general vibe? Let’s dust off the book description.“When Donovan, basketball star and notorious heartthrob of the Campus Drivers, realizes the damage his womanizing behavior has caused, he decides to become the perfect boyfriend. And who better to coach him than romance-obsessed, all-around good girl Carrie? Carrie wants nothing to do with Donovan. She’d much rather lose herself in the heroes from her romance novels. They’re uncomplicated and drama-free, just the way she likes it. If Donovan wants her help, he’ll have to endure a tough, no-nonsense crash course in romance. But one can’t just become a romance hero overnight—especially when reality refuses to stick to the script.”

Time to do some math. According to IMDb, Campus Drivers’ first season was filmed from October 2025 to January 2026. It then dropped in November. This is a pretty fast turnaround, so assuming the show is renewed and filming takes place in early 2027, we could be looking at a late 2027 or early 2028 release date. So, about a year from now (or a little longer).

Tay has returned to her country music roots while promoting her CMA Single of the Year nominee from the Toy Story 5 soundtrack, “I Knew It, I Knew You.” And man, it’s been a while, because this also marks her first performance at the awards show in 13 years.

If that wasn’t enough 13s to convince you this is a legit T. Swift announcement, the last time she graced the CMA stage was to perform an orchestral version of “Red” in 2013.

Naturally, Swifties are already piecing together potential clues for the night. Given all the 13s surrounding her announcement and her return to country music since fully transitioning to pop following her last CMA appearance, fans are convinced something big is coming. #6767. The Secret to Kendall Jenner’s ‘Me Mode’

The model and founder shares her favorite ways to unwind and how the Therabody TheraFace Mask Glo fits into her nightly routine.

For Kendall Jenner, “Me Mode” is a time to unplug from the daily demands of work and social obligations and simply be still. “I have a really busy mind, so switching off has never come naturally to me,” says the model and entrepreneur. “I’ve had to build little rituals that help me slow down.”

One of those rituals involves Therabody’s TheraFace Mask Glo, an LED face mask that bridges skincare with wellness. Delivering full LED coverage across the entire face, including the lips and under eyes, the cordless device features 504 red, infrared, and blue LEDs, plus a relaxing scalp massage to help relieve tension. Best of all, treatment time is only 12 minutes, giving Jenner a quick, easy, and much-needed pause from her busy schedule.

“After a long day, it’s sweatpants, my dogs, and zero plans,” Jenner says. “Putting on the Mask Glo has kind of become my signal that the day is over. For those 12 minutes, my brain finally goes quiet.”

One thing Jenner loves about the TheraFace Mask Glo is how effortlessly it fits into her routine. “It’s hands-free, so I just put it on and do whatever I feel like that night,” Jenner says. “Sometimes that’s music or journaling, and a lot of the time it’s doing absolutely nothing. The scalp massage is my favorite part. It feels less like a skincare step and more like actually relaxing.”

It offers legitimate skincare benefits, too. Backed by the largest clinical study of any LED mask on the market, the TheraFace Mask Glo was shown to reduce under-eye wrinkles and fine lines, enhance radiance, and lift sagging skin in as little as eight weeks.

The TheraFace Mask Glo offers three LED settings to target different skin concerns. Red light helps improve the appearance of fine lines, wrinkles, and dark spots, while red and infrared light work together to boost collagen production. Blue light targets acne-causing bacteria, helping prevent future breakouts.

The key is consistent use, which Jenner has found easy to maintain. “If a routine is too complicated, I’m not going to stick with it, and I’ve learned that consistency matters more than anything,” she says. “With the Mask Glo, I pick red, red and infrared, or blue light depending on how my skin is acting, and that’s it. It’s easy enough that I actually use it, which is half the battle for me. It comes with me when I travel now, too.”

The LED lights are placed across the entire face, even covering overlooked areas like the mouth and eyes. The preset treatment runs for 12 minutes, cycling through four minutes of each light setting. Four-minute individual treatments are also available.

Combining advanced skincare and relaxation into one simple step, the TheraFace Mask Glo is ideal for anyone who wants to level up their nighttime routine with minimal effort. As Jenner has learned over the years, less is more when it comes to beauty and wellness.

“I’ve been sitting in makeup chairs since I was 14, so I went through a phase of trying everything,” she says. “Now I do a lot less, and I’m more intentional about what I put on my skin. I try to give it a break when I can, and I’ll use the Mask Glo before glam on shoot days.”

Jenner’s nighttime routine is just one aspect in living a more holistic lifestyle. “Wellness for me is mostly about balance now: being at the barn with my horses, getting outside, and protecting my nights,” she says. “My friends make fun of me for going to bed so early, but I’m not changing.”

#6764. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.
