#6763. Even a Gay Assassin Can’t Escape the Damsel-in-Distress Trope on Black Doves

It’s not *better* but at least it’s different?

In many ways, Black Doves is a platonic love story between a spy, Helen (Keira Knightley), and an assassin, Sam (Ben Whishaw). But the Netflix series has plenty of room for sex and romance. You’d expect that, right? It wouldn’t be a good spy story without some kind of spice. But while Black Doves plays along with a lot of familiar spy romance tropes, it does one thing differently: it does them with men. Spoilers for Black Doves season 2 ahead.

When you watch a lot of movies and television shows within the same genre, whether for work, continuing education, or just pure love of the game, you tend to pick up on some common tropes. Sometimes, like becoming aware of a friend’s bad habits for the first time, it’s annoyingly difficult to un-notice them.

One of my least favorite tropes in the action genre, or set of tropes really, has to do with how the spy and/or assassin’s love interest invariably gets treated. These women, because they’re practically always women, are kept in the dark and therefore lack agency. The romances are inherently toxic because they lack trust! Then, the second the bad guys learn about them, these women get kidnapped or killed or otherwise put in danger to motivate and/or devastate the male hero. Boo, I say! Boring! Sexist, even! These women are not characters. They are plot devices.

In Black Doves season 1, we learn that Sam was once in a relationship with a man named Michael (Omari Douglas) that he maintained by lying about what he did for a living. When Sam’s real job follows him home, so to speak, Michael is put in a traumatic and distressing situation. Someone is shooting at him, his boyfriend is a stone-cold killer... and his boyfriend lied to him. When Michael returns, briefly, in season 2, it’s the last thing that seems to have sucked the most.

Then, in season 2, Sam tries something safer. He engages in a sexual situationship with a hotel concierge named Jerry (Samuel Barnett). On paper, it’s perfect for someone like him. Jerry is into him, but not too much. There’s no chance of them being seen in public together, because he works at the same place they hook up. It’s genuinely sweet to see Jerry try to get Sam to open up. But the second that Sam does, and invites him on a proper date, the bad guys swoop in and use Jerry as leverage. Not only does the poor concierge die for the plot, but Sam is forced to fridge his own love interest.

Is this now homophobic, instead of sexist? I would tentatively say no, for a few reasons. I covered the “Bury Your Gays” moment circa 2016, and I don’t think this qualifies there either. You can’t really say that the show thinks gay men don’t have agency, and are only fit to be plot devices when Sam, a gay man, is co-lead. And if Sam was the only queer character in the series, his inability to find happiness might be a problem; but two other assassins, Williams (Ella Lily Hyland) and Eleanor (Gabrielle Creevy), are both women and gleefully infatuated with each other. The “murder lesbians,” as another character on the show decided to label them, are in the least toxic romantic relationship on Black Doves. So, that’s something!

For what it’s worth, Helen’s husband in the series, Wallace (Andrew Buchan), is also kept in the dark for his own protection and then put in danger to motivate Helen at the end of season 2. Plus, her extramarital love interest in season 1, Jason (Andrew Koji), was quite literally killed for the plot. This really is a dangerous show for men. It’s about damn time? Forget I said that.

It is darkly refreshing (now that we’re in Woke 3, I’ve decided we can call things refreshing again) to see these tropes done on Black Doves in a way that balances the scales. I found the dozens of articles explaining why women feel safe watching “M/M romance” like Heated Rivalry to be condescending at times, and therefore I’m hesitant to admit that it’s nice for me as a woman to watch a story with these tropes that so many men seem to enjoy. I still don’t see the appeal. Nobody should be a plot device, regardless of gender. But I’m slightly less bored, and against all odds, rooting for Sam to find some peace and a lasting romance of his own.

#6762. Birkenstock Clogs Are Easier to Dress Up Than You Think

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Birkenstock clogs have never really gone out of style, but when skinny jeans were replaced with wide-leg and bootcut jeans, they firmly reclaimed their place on the shoe rack. Now that sneakers go way more places than the gym and the grocery store, you should give your Birkenstocks another glance and see your Bostons as a fashion opportunity.

Plus, Birkenstocks have gone far beyond the taupe Boston—the label has made clog variations like the Mary Jane-inspired Maria and modern Loma. Or if you’re still partial to the Boston, you can get the clog style in a variety of cool colors, like Oyster and Thyme, and variations, like big buckles or braided straps.

If you’re not sure how you can transition your Bostons from your throw-on, afterthought shoe, we’re here to help. Below, I’ve compiled some outfit inspiration for you and the pieces you need to re-create it.

Birkenstocks at the office? Absolutely—they are clogs after all, and people wear sneakers to the office all the time. Why wear those flats that give you blisters when your Bostons are right there? Pair them with a coordinating trench and white wide-leg jeans (because we haven’t been following that archaic white-after-Labor-Day rule all fall).

Bernardo Belted Stretch Cotton Trench Coat

Even supermodel and Guest In Residence founder Gigi Hadid can’t resist a comfy pair of Birks. For a perfectly executed, effortless, and casual look, pair your Bostons with a graphic tee and bootcut jeans. Create some visual interest by tying your cardigan or sweatshirt like Hadid, instead of simply around your neck. Now that Bostons are everywhere again, opt for a color like Oyster to set yourself apart from the Taupe pack.

Birkenstocks with a matching sweater set? Hear me out—I’m going to cite stylist Allison Bornstein’s “wrong shoe theory.” It’s been around for a while, but wearing a historically “wrong” shoe with an outfit is often so, so right. You probably have never thought about pairing your Birkenstocks with a skirt, but if you go monochrome, like I’m suggesting, it creates a visually interesting outfit. I suggest opting for Birkenstock’s Maria, a Mary Jane-esque variation on the Boston, if you want a sleeker silhouette.

Everyone has a comfy errands uniform—myself included—but instead of slipping on those track pants or leggings with your Birks for the umpteenth time, maybe elevate your look with some silk pants and a designer suede tote or deviate from the Birkenstock Boston. I recommend checking out the Loma, a new wrap-style clog silhouette from the brand, a modern and playful take on the classic Boston.

Take a page out of this off-duty model’s playbook. Don’t be afraid to mix neutral tones, like cream, black, and gray. The mélange of textures—the suede clogs, colored denim, heather polo, and quilted bag—creates an effortlessly cozy look. Trust me, you’ll be wearing this on repeat on the weekends this fall and winter.

#6760. Dialed In: All the Time in the World

Accutron’s bold new Horizon Chronograph unites all the best elevated complications in watchmaking in a single watch at a fantastic price.

Every now and then, a watch comes along that demolishes all your preconceived notions of the way things are in watchmaking. This week, Accutron released the Horizon, an entirely new timepiece (in two colorways, blue and black) that positively bristles with complications normally associated with five- and six-figure watches and at a very attractive price to boot.

It's a bold move from the brand that created the very first fully electronic watch back in 1960. With that model, Accutron debuted its tuning fork technology, by which a traditional mechanical watch escapement, with all its susceptibility to friction and gravity, was replaced with a tiny metal tuning fork powered by electricity via a built-in transistor. What made the tuning fork ground-breaking was the fact that, under power, it vibrated at a constant and continuous rate. And it made the aptly named Accutron (hence the name) accurate to plus or minus one second a month, an unimagined degree of accuracy at that point, and, frankly, for a long time after too.

Back to the Horizon. First up in a litany of nifty and rather mind-blowing gizmos is the fact that this Master Complication tells you the time not only with the hands of the watch in the conventional way, but also with sound. This watch is a minute repeater, chiming the current time in hours, then quarter hours, then minutes at the press of a button. Derived in the 18th century from the need to know the time at night a good century before electric light and SuperLuminova markers made their appearance, the minute repeater could—once you got the hang of it—tell you the exact time even in pitch darkness. But it's a complication so rare and expensive to make, that we honestly can't think of a minute repeater ever produced at such a compelling price.

The bells and whistles don't stop there. With three sub dials in glittery black sandstone that looks like the night sky) set into a granular dial made from Denchu, another granular sandstone, all beneath the conventional center hands, this watch is both a Perpetual Calendar and Chronograph. It features the day and the date, the leap year, the phases of the moon, a second time zone and a day/night indicator, all packed into an elegant and sporty 41.5mm stainless steel case. This, by the way, is made in a superior 904L steel with a matching steel bracelet, an alloy known for its deep luster when polished, as well as its resistance to corrosion and pitting. Underlying all this style is the Horizon's substance; the watch is driven by a quartz movement that offers excellent accuracy.

If you want to understand the meaning of time, you only have to gaze into the Horizon's dial, which breaks it all down for you in a very cool package. Reading it will keep you busy for days.

#6757. Looking for a New Winter Perfume? These 4 Zara Vanilla Scents Smell So Expensive

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

There’s a reason vanilla perfumes are so popular. They’re no longer the sickly-sweet, sugar-loaded compositions you might remember. Today they’re much more complex—the creaminess of the note is balanced with more sophisticated whiffs of florals, woods, and amber. This new generation of vanilla-forward fragrances feels rounded, full-bodied, and, perhaps most importantly, expensive.

Sure, many of these complex scents come with a hefty price tag. But luckily, Zara has an entire collection dedicated to these deep vanilla perfumes, and there is an array of options that are all ideal for a comforting winter scent. With every spritz, you’ll smell how distinct each one is. In some, vanilla sits quietly at the base, gently rounding off sweeter or floral notes. In others, it takes center stage, creating a creamy, decadent throw.

Whether you like your vanilla subtle and soft for an everyday spritz, or bold and woody for after-hours, there’s a budget-friendly Zara perfume calling your name.

Notes: Chantilly cream, jasmine, sweet tonkaMood: A gourmand scent that’s indulgent and warm but never sickly

The truest vanilla of them all, Dark Romance is the place to start if you’re after a traditionally sweet, creamy—but not sickly—vanilla fragrance. The base note is sweet tonka, which creates that toasty, caramel-y base that sits on your skin, while notes of jasmine and Chantilly cream linger with a velvety, milky sillage. It’s the perfect everyday fragrance; one we envision misting on a cashmere sweater on a chilly night.

Notes: Apricot blossom, jasmine sambac, vanillaMood: A floral vanilla that’s creamy and comforting

Zara’s Hypnotic Vanilla smells exactly as it sounds. Decadent from the first spritz, it instantly envelops you in this golden, buttery cloud of vanilla. Initially, it’s sweet, but then the apricot blossom and jasmine sambac come through, giving it a floral and slightly powdery edge. If Dark Romance is the one you’d wear for a cozy night in, Hypnotic Vanilla is its older sister you’ll reach for for a night out. It’s a great option if you can never choose between gourmands and florals.

Notes: Salty coconut, jasmine, vanilla bloom, amberMood: A full-bodied gourmand that’s creamy, sweet, a little spicy, and seductive

With Immortal Vanilla, you’ll be surprised it only costs $39. While Zara’s perfumes have impressive sillage, this one truly does linger, so you’re bound to receive many compliments. On paper, it has notes of coconut, jasmine, amber, and vanilla. But IRL, they come together to create something that’s comforting, sensual, and ever so slightly creamy and salty. After one spritz, you’ll want to douse yourself in it again, and again, and again.

Notes: Wild orchid, black amber, vanilla Mood: A deep, sophisticated scent that balances vanilla with a woody base

If you want your vanilla perfume to smell like an old-fashioned, New York-style jazz bar, Daring Vanilla is for you. Of the four scents, this is the one that comes closest to a traditional oud fragrance, with naturally smoky, woody notes balanced by wild orchid and vanilla, which lend a warm, subtly sweet throw. It’s the kind of fragrance you’d slip over an LBD, slide into some killer high heels and head out for the night.

Through her work, she has interviewed leading dermatologists, makeup artists and industry experts to bring readers trusted advice and product recommendations. #6756. Justin Theroux Took Me on a Nearly Perfect Trip to Japan. Here’s the Itinerary.

“There’s tourism, and there’s travel,” the actor says. After four days across Tokyo and Kyoto, he reveals exactly what that means.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Justin Theroux carefully holds the brush perpendicular to the sheet of paper. For the past 20-odd minutes, he’s been learning how to press, glide, press a single Japanese kanji character: 永, eternity.

The 55-year-old actor has been practicing each one methodically, including a particularly tricky “airplane” maneuver in which he must hold the brush outward, away from his chest, and guide it gently across the sheet to make a tail that broadens as it travels before ending on a punctuating point at which he must deftly lift his hand away.

Despite praise from his instructor, a renowned Japanese calligrapher called Mamimozi—“Sugoi!” she exclaims, which means amazing—Theroux groans at his subpar airplane attempts. He’s not satisfied with good enough, especially now that the stakes have heightened: He’s been handed a thicker sheet that, once it’s inked with 永, will be framed as a gift to take back home to New York after this trip to Japan.

On this particular journey, he’s partnered with the Luxury Collection, a wide portfolio of award-winning hotels and resorts like the Gritti Palace in Venice and the Royal Hawaiian in Honolulu, to curate experiences like this calligraphy session as a gateway beyond Japan’s typical touristy activities.

“I always feel bad when you see your buses pull up to places and you see people file out, they take pictures, then they get back on the bus, and they go somewhere else,” he tells Esquire. “Anytime you can get access behind walls to a place—that’s what truly unlocks any travel experience.”

The actor has visited Japan numerous times and, over the course of those visits, has connected deeply with Japanese culture. As a “global explorer” and face of the Luxury Collection Expeditions campaign, Theroux has shaped those life-changing travel experiences into two itineraries.

First there’s the Global Explorer Edit: Tokyo with Justin Theroux, based out of the five-star Prince Gallery Tokyo Kioicho hotel, which offers panoramic views of Mount Fuji when the weather’s right. If travelers want to branch out beyond Tokyo to Japan’s imperial capital, the Global Explorer Edit: Kyoto with Justin Theroux offers a different perspective of the Land of the Rising Sun. Guests stay at Hotel the Mitsui, a Michelin three-key accommodation, where modern comforts and historic sensibilities collide for a beyond-memorable stay.

There’s a noticeable theme to Theroux’s itineraries, visible even in the mundane experience of day-to-day Japan. Everyone here, from the citizen on the street to the service worker to the artist, aims to do their very best, much like Theroux does as he makes his first attempt at 永 on the thicker sheet. The streets are litter-free, despite—or because of—the lack of public garbage cans, and the public transit is perpetually, impeccably, on time.

Though Theroux fails to land the airplane this time, there are a few more sheets lying around, a few more chances to try again—and, afterward, a few more stops along his itinerary.

It’s late morning on a partly cloudy September Saturday when Theroux and company—press, PR, friends, guides—leave the Prince Gallery for a speakeasy hidden on the third story of a nondescript building in Tokyo’s Shibuya district. The calligraphy experience awaits inside.

After Theroux decides on his best 永 to frame, Mamimozi, the artist, prepares to perform. She steadies a brush as big as her forearm before making decisive motions, quickly and smoothly, across a sheet the size of a side table. Her movement is entrancing, engrossing—and then, snap, it’s over.

“How does she decide she’s pleased?” Theroux asks Mamimozi’s translator, who passes the question on.

Normally, Mamimozi would do 100 of these before she decides on the best one, the translator relays. This particular character, though? She’s about “92 percent” happy.

“If I ever get to 100 percent,” Mamimozi says through the translator, “that’s the end of my pursuit.” She’s always trying to grow as an artist.

Theroux is presented with his calligraphy in a sturdy frame. Mamimozi having taught Theroux with this particular kanji—永, eternity—suddenly makes a whole lot of sense. “I approach everything artistically, whether it’s writing or acting, as a monastic experience where you’re never going to be completely pleased with everything you do—but you’re always striving for something,” he tells Esquire.

The next pursuit? Scoring fashionable vintage wear, which Tokyo supplies in high volume. Theroux’s Tokyo Edit ensures that travelers are able to take advantage of the spoils of one of the world’s premier fashion markets, focused on four of his favorite shops: Portration, Visvim, Old Joe, and Brackets, all in close proximity in Shibuya. Theroux and Co. then attend the Nanzuka contemporary-art gallery, also in Shibuya, but travelers should expect a visit on the earlier side of their itinerary.

If shopping makes guests hungry, Theroux’s itinerary recommends Yoroniku, an omakase that serves world-class Wagyu. But on this particular trip, Theroux and Co. first head to Bar Centifolia, where “samurai bartender” Yuzo Komai puts on a show that’s earned him the title of world’s best cocktail artist.

When an order for a smoked old-fashioned is received, the house lights go down, leaving a spotlight on Komai. He unleashes a big blue fan and blows air on a large, perfectly clear ice cube to sweat it just a little before unsheathing a katana and chopping it into a trapezoidal prism, which he slides neatly into a rocks glass. Next he pours in a sweet tincture and Beyoncé’s signature SirDavis whiskey and stirs them together with a cocktail spoon. Then he places a circular cut of wood atop and lights the whole damn thing aflame with a blowtorch.

A billow of smoke arises from the concoction, which tastes of sweet and smoky caramelized popcorn. For those less adventurous, the bar owns dozens of rare Japanese whiskies, so delicate and floral and complex that they make the perfect nightcap to relax the shoulders after a dizzying, rewarding day.

To begin the Kyoto itinerary, Theroux finds himself sitting cross-legged in the home studio of third-generation lacquerer Nishimura Keikou. His bowls and cups—which go for $650 a pop—begin their lives as a base of wood whittled to a thickness of less than a millimeter before he begins to layer lacquer, a brownish sap that’s extracted from the tree of the same name. It’s a precious resource, “like the blood of a human,” Keikou says through a translator. The trees do not give much, and once depleted, they die. A single piece of lacquerware can take an entire year to craft, as each of the requisite 14 layers must dry completely before another can be shellacked over top.

Unlike Mamimozi, the calligrapher, Keikou doesn’t consider himself an artist. He doesn’t create because he has an innate need to express; he creates because clients request his goods. Theroux feels a connection nonetheless, especially when Keikou mentions the plight of the woodworkers who make the base of his pieces. They’re behind the camera, so to speak, receiving little glory or credit. Theroux, who is not just an actor but a writer, seems to internalize this as Keikou hands him a bowl as a gift.

Next up on Theroux’s Kyoto itinerary: sushi. His preferred spot, Otachidokoro Sushi Ki, is a stand-up joint where guests can order à la carte or in a set menu. Guests are encouraged to eat with their hands here, which they can rinse between bites using spouts of water that trickle from below a raised edge on the sushi bar.

Theroux’s sushi artisan, befuddlingly, is an Italian immigrant from just outside Milan. How the hell did he get here?

“I came here by plane,” Francesco jokes. Seven years ago, at 27 years old, he committed himself to making sushi at its highest level. Where to go to complete his training? Kyoto, of course, without a return ticket. Now he’s serving Justin Theroux scallop over soy-sauce-soaked rice with a sprinkle of salt and a zest of lime on top.

With bellies full, it’s time for a Zen garden tour. Kyoto is home to many temples and shrines and gardens both Buddhist and Shinto, Japan’s two national religions. But this garden—situated in the Konchi-in Buddhist temple and named, simply, the Crane and Turtle Garden after the shapes of its central rock formations—doesn’t get much foot traffic. It’s more peaceful than the bigger, more popular tourist-mobbed shrines, ideal for a thoughtful traveler like Theroux.

It’s probably “97 out of 100” in terms of popularity, says tour guide Andrew William. Like the sushi chef from earlier, William is not from Japan. He is from Maryland, not too far from Washington, D.C., where Theroux grew up. Like Francesco, he uprooted himself to perfect his art elsewhere, though instead of making sushi, he designs and builds gardens. He’s worked on big projects in the past, including Ben Stiller’s estate on the north shore of Kauai, Hawaii. William’s pursuit, however, belies the reality of chasing dreams: They often turn to nightmares.

William once wanted to be head groundskeeper of this idyllic garden. But it’s not so easy for foreigners to rise in Japanese hierarchies. He claims to have suffered abuse at the hands of his employers and endured long working hours for low pay—“a lot of bad things to maintain beauty,” he says, still apparently chewing on the irony.

Theroux is no stranger to that feeling. Sometimes, he says of acting, “you’ll have a miserable experience that’s received very well. And you think, God, I was so miserable on that job. And yet the product was so good, ’cause so many craftsmen are bringing their talents to bear on it. The editor really helped it, the music really helped it, the director was very good—although they might have been an asshole.”

Theroux and William sit side by side on a broad wooden step that leads into the temple and overlooks the amorphous stony shapes of the crane and the turtle. He and Theroux are perched, William says, on what Buddhist philosophy considers the ship of life. Out directly in front of them is an expanse of pebbles raked into a motif of alternating swirls and straight lines, an ocean of respective rough and smooth seas. On the other side, the crane and turtle await, representing the harmony that anticipates us at the end of our earthly journey.

There is still an evening of activities to go: A visit to the temple. Matcha and handmade sweets in the temple’s tearoom. An intimate geisha dinner complete with drinks, cultural conversation, and traditional Japanese food. All of which prove to be enriching experiences for travelers. Yet it is here, facing the crane and turtle, where visitors can meditate on their journey and appreciate the unique experience on which they’ve embarked.

“There’s tourism, and there’s travel,” Theroux says. “And I think they are two totally different things.”

Tourism is hoofing it from place to place, checking boxes. Seen that, did this. But travel, Theroux’s travel, is different. It’s about meeting people, making friends beside the worker at the front desk or the technician at the spa, and engaging with their lives, their realities, on a deeper level.

“Anytime you can have access to something unique or an area of interest—whether that’s craftsmanship, arts, music, dance, knives, enamelware, things like that—you’re always going to get a little peek into a country or city that you didn’t know existed,” he says.

Like all his endeavors, Theroux’s Japan Expeditions aren’t perfect—if they were, there’d be no reason for visitors to come back—but they’re as close to perfect as it gets.

Both Justin Theroux’s Tokyo and Kyoto Global Explorer Edits are available for booking on the Luxury Collection Expeditions website. The Tokyo Edit is a daylong experience that costs $9,505, while the Kyoto Edit, also a daylong experience, costs $13,145. That price includes a guest, a one-night hotel stay, a private guide, and transportation.

#6753. The 6 Smartphones to Buy in 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We don’t care if you’re on team Apple or Android. We’ve found you the phone to upgrade to.

You’ve landed here, so let me guess: You’re an iPhone person and you want a new iPhone. Or ... you’re an Android person, dedicated to your brand of choice already, be it Google or Samsung. Here’s the thing: Your choice of phone says a lot about you, but realistically you’ve already locked yourself into choosing between the same three phones all your friends are getting. We are humans, so our brains are finely tuned to love habits. The result is not too many people want to adjust to a new way of interfacing with daily life every other year; they just want the same thing. There’s a chance you are the rare third kind of person who wants a change. In all three cases, there’s good news. You kinda already know what you want (and don’t want) out of a smartphone.

My fellow editors and I have tried the latest in what Apple, Samsung, and Google have to offer, and have determined for ourselves which are the greatest. This year, the biggest shake-up to the race is foldable phones. Not entirely a new category, but the iPhone Duo is the capstone on a past 12 months that have legitimized the form across both Apple and Android devices. You’ll be paying a premium, and not all the software is properly optimized yet, but the tech is finally there. For everyone else, there’s still the new pro model iPhone or the latest A series Pixel for the value hunters. Regardless of whether you’re after a bold reinvention, or just an improved camera and a slightly shinier display, you’ll find something to love in these six smartphones.

I’ve made vocal arguments in favor of going against the grain and getting a Windows device instead of a MacBook, but at the end of the day it’s all too easy to stick with it if you’re on team iPhone already. The 18 Pro is the iPhone to get if you’re a daily power user, on your phone 24/7 for both work and personal reasons. It’s got a glorious 120-hertz-refresh-rate retina screen, exactly the smoothness and crispness you’ve come to expect from a pro level phone.

It’s not merely the one we’ve seen the most in the wild, it’s the best bet for an iPhone that will last you another four years. And while we can't say we love the new design, it’s been easy to get used to. And functionally, it’s the iPhone Pro we know and love. It is fast, it’s got a smooth 120 hertz screen and it has one of the best cameras you can get on a phone. If there’s one upgrade we truly feel each time we get a new phone, it’s the camera. This year’s is no exception.

Compared with the other Android offerings out there, the Pixel 11 Pro is in elite territory. It’s not technically as beefy as a Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra or ZFold 8 Ultra, but it’s notably a lot cheaper than either of those phones.

It’s fast and it’s got that smooth refresh rate a high-end phone should. Best of all, holy shit is the camera something else? It can take one incredible photo after another and it’s probably equipped with the best selfie camera on a phone. Our editors who’ve tested this one also won’t shut up about how much better Gemini is than every other AI assistant. Finally, a “smart” feature that lives up to the moniker. And compared to the equivalent iPhones and Samsung phones, the battery life reigns supreme—even if, as a trade-off, it takes the longest to charge.

If you’re of the Android persuasion, it’s easy to see the iPhone 18 Pro as the other team’s Google Pixel 11 Pro, instead of the other way around. On a pure specs level it’s a better phone—just probably not better enough to get Apple diehards over the OS gap. Serious sarcasm aside, it’s not the iPhone users who need this phone. It’s everyone currently suffering on an older Pixel model or, worse, some no-name Android phone.

It was only a couple of years ago you could read me advising you not to buy a renewed iPhone. But with how expensive all technology has become recently, there is now a wide enough gulf between something like this and a new iPhone to make buying refurbished worth the cost savings. Instead of buying a 17e new for $700, pick up this refurbished 16e—it’s half the price, and you won’t be living too far in the past.

The 16e is pretty much everything a phone should be and nothing extraneous. This phone is light, yet durable. It’s got the Action Button from the 16 lineup but does lack the Camera Control.

The other omission of note is the lack of MagSafe functionality. The 16e supports wireless Qi charging, but it does not magnetize the way every iPhone since the 12 (barring the SE line) has. Once again, we don’t think this matters to the core audience for this phone, so it’s less of a flaw and more of a way Apple has honed in on exactly the audience it wants to hit for this one.

For $600, this is the most affordable big-name smartphone. There are plenty of Androids in the $200 to $300 range, but by and large they aren’t meant to last. They are poorly made stopgaps to get you to something like the Pixel 10a. Right now for only $400, this is the best value you can get on a brand-new smartphone.

It has a lot of what we love from the newest Pixel phones—including the latest OS features like Gemini and the invaluable Call Screening feature that automatically detects spam. And while the front-facing camera isn’t mind-blowing compared with the 10 series Pixels, it does still rock a very impressive wide-angle back camera that offers more variation than the e series of iPhones.

The iPhone Duo is the most innovative and groundbreaking phone release of the year. We said as much in our gadget awards and editor Luke Gullory has been giving his updates along the way. He’s tested and tested, and the verdict is in. This one is a keeper and, most importantly, it’s the best foldable phone to hit the market yet.

The genius touch here is the aspect ratio. For the Duo, Apple went with a √2:1 (or 1.414:1) aspect ratio. That is slightly different from the other foldable, but the subtle change makes a difference. On the Duo, the small outside screen exactly mirrors the size of half the large inner screen, but it also exactly mirrors the total aspect ratio of the entire large inner screen. The matte screen seems odd at first, but it’s the solution Apple has found to showing any creasing and, once again, this gives it that design edge. There’s a reason people start to care when Apple does it.

I’ll also note it has the same chipset and specs as the 18 Pro, including RAM. Normally, this would put it on par power-wise, but the real-world performance lags behind due to the amount of thermal throttling needed to pull off the design miracle of the form factor.

If you want a flip phone, you can shop vintage for pennies but when it comes to using those like daily phones, the truth is they don’t support modern cellular networks. They are as good as a toy. But if you still want that flip phone experience, Samsung’s most affordable new folding phone gives you something of the sort.

Don’t get me wrong, the Z Flip8 is still expensive, starting at $1,200. But compared to all the other foldables from Samsung and Google, it is technically the least expensive one. That’s largely because it’s got the least amount of screen space. Really it’s just a normal-sized Galaxy phone with a front-screen, not something that has a unique aspect ratio like the Duo or the Galaxy Z Fold 8 phones. On a specs level, it’s on par with these phones. Crisp 120 hertz screen, solid front and rear cameras, and all the Android app support you’d want. Really, though, if you’re committing it’s a commitment to having a wacky, folding flip phone that you can set up like a tent or show off all the different camera modes.

#6752. ‘DTWS’ Fans Are Divided Over Connor Wood and Rylee Arnold’s “Recycled” Dance-Off Choreography

Was it a clever callback or just too familiar?

I never thought I’d say this, but this week’s episode of Dancing With the Stars was a tough watch. Hear me out—it was only because my faves, Maura Higgins and Connor Wood, competed in a bonus dance-off to Sheryl Crow’s “All I Wanna Do.” I just couldn’t choose between these charming celebs. Sue me!

That being said, it was a tight race between the stars and their pro partners, Rylee Arnold and Mark Ballas. Team Tink won in the end thanks to two extra points from the judging panel, but DWTS fans seem divided over who really deserved the win after the scores were given.

“ANYONE who thought connor had a chance i hope you're feeling real embarrassed MAURA ATE HIM UP SOOOO HARD,” one person wrote, while another said, “I don’t even care that they lost (whatever) but I like watching Rylee and Connor WAYYY more.”

After the performance, Carrie Ann Inaba criticized Rylee and Connor for incorporating more country-inspired moves into their cha-cha.

“So much fun, both of you! It’s like the battle of fringe dance,” she said, before telling Connor and Rylee, “You had a lot of country line dancing. You threw that back in, which was fun for the audience, but I don’t know. It’s not a cha-cha.”

Naturally, fans took issue with Carrie Ann’s comments. “What’s the point of picking a country song if you don’t want them to incorporate that into their dance. The feedback made no sense,” another viewer said.

If that wasn’t enough to keep the debate alive, others pointed out how similar Rylee and Connor’s dance was to their week 1 cha-cha to another country song, “I Like It, I Love It” by Tim McGraw.

However, longtime fans know that repurposing choreography is the name of the game when it comes to DWTS.

One commenter on Reddit explained, “Truth be told, all the pros recycle dances for the dance-offs, which is why the concept has never really worked for me or been particularly entertaining. However, those Hee Haw moves definitely threw the entire dance-off off, and I’m glad CAI called it out. You could tell she absolutely hated every second of it.”

Either way, it doesn’t seem like Connor or Maura were phased by the online chatter after their dance-off. They actually dropped a series of comments supporting each other’s performances.

“So much fun with you guys @fibula @ryleearnold1,” Maura wrote, to which Connor responded, “we had too much damn fun I love you all.”

The podcaster even jokingly chimed in when a fan claimed Maura “cleared” him, saying, “agree that’s my queen.”

And that’s why these two are my faves—can’t wait to see them in the semifinals next week!

#6751. The Best Apple Deals to Shop Before Black Friday

AirTags and AirPods are already up to 20 percent off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Black Friday is arguably the best time of the year to shop for Apple products. While the tech giant doesn’t run sales of its own, other retailers often offer their own markdowns during this holiday sales event. Get a head start now, because you won’t need to wait until the holiday weekend to score markdowns on AirPods, MacBooks, Apple Watches, and other popular Apple products. Amazon is already offering over a dozen lead-up deals. Here, we’ll detail which Apple discounts are worth shopping early and which products you should wait to buy.

If you’re going to shop one product early, make it Apple AirTags, which dropped to their lowest price ever in time for holiday travel. Individual trackers are $23, but the discounted four-pack brings the cost down to $20 apiece.

The new AirPods 5 and trendy EarPods remain at full price (for now), but other headphones are already discounted. Currently, you can save $50 on the noise-canceling AirPods Pro 3 and $100 on the over-the-ear AirPods Max 2. These products could drop further during Amazon’s Turkey 12 sale—the e-tailer’s name for this year’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday event—but they could also sell out, as they have in years past.

If you’re in need of a new laptop, now’s a good time to make the purchase. Select MacBook models are already discounted. The 2026 Pro is still full price, but the 2026 Air is up to 15 percent off. The budget-friendly MacBook Neo hasn’t gone on sale yet, though we suspect it will be discounted for the first time during Amazon’s Turkey 12 sales event.

Apple Watch shoppers may want to wait, depending on the model they’re after. The Series 12 and SE 3 haven’t budged, but you can already save up to $100 on the giftable Ultra 4.

All three iPad models are currently marked down on Amazon. Based on past price history, there’s a small chance the Pro, Air, and classic 11-inch iPad will see a slightly deeper discount during the official sale, but we don’t expect prices to drop significantly. We’re also seeing strong early deals on Apple accessories like keyboards and chargers.

#6749. Amazon Has Some Incredible Deals on Levi’s Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It's easy for me, the style writer, to say you shouldn't determine whether or not you buy something based on how much it is (or is not) on sale. I know how the real world works, though. I write about denim all the time, and I use every opportunity I get to recommend the fancy stuff. Honestly though, I spend most days in a pair of $40 Wranglers that I bought on sale for $25, because I also am a regular American trying to get by. Used to be, good denim only cost a few bucks. Now, it's lower quality and costs more money. Reproductions of what used to be American working clothes now easily cost $300 or more. C'est la vie.

But I'm not here to talk about all that. I'm here to say like with my Wranglers, it's OK to wear cheap jeans. You can still be stylish if your pants cost $50. This month’s early Black Friday Amazon deals on Levi’s prove it.

We've got the 505, the modern Levi's fit I think works for most men, that's selling for between $45 and $55—depending on the wash you want and size you need. The 517, Levi's take on a Western cut, is going for a discount. And the 501—which I don't love in its modern iteration, but cannot deny the popularity of—is hovering just below $50, depending on the size and wash. Depending on the jeans you buy, you’re getting a discount of between 19 and 43 percent for the best cheap denim out there. If you're looking for a new jacket, which Levi's still makes decent versions of, Amazon has deals on two sherpa trucker jackets. Go with the classic denim for $63 or opt for the corduroy for $85.

Alright, that's enough explaining. Pick what speaks to you. Buy it. Then, go forth a new man with refreshed style.

#6747. Spice Up Your Mani with Cinnamon Brown Nails This Fall

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

At this point, we all know that a brown manicure is a fall staple. Even as I type this right now, I’m wearing one that’s a week old (in DND Spiced Brown, if you’re wondering). Espresso nails? Love. Chocolate brown nails? Yep. Chai latte nails? A lighter way to do things. But, it turns out we haven’t exhausted every way to wear the shade quite yet. The latest brown look to get on my radar is cinnamon nails, the warm, spicy take on the fall-friendly color. And honestly, with the holidays around the corner, the smell of cinnamon is sure to be wafting through my kitchen for the next couple months, so the timing couldn’t be better.

These warm, spicy nails combine rich brown with subtle red and copper undertones for a cozy, cinnamon-inspired shade that feels tailor-made for autumn. Ahead, with the help of celebrity nail artist Stephanie Stone (she’s Sally Hansen’s nail style director and has worked with lots of your favorite stars—ahem, Charli XCX) I’ll break down everything you need to know about trending cinnamon brown nails, including plenty of inspo pics, tips for getting the look, and polish recommendations.

As the name suggests, cinnamon brown nails are inspired by the warm, reddish-brown hue of the spice itself. It’s warmer than a classic brown and less red than a terracotta, sitting right in between the two. While espresso and chocolate brown nails tend to lean darker and cooler, these bring some toasty and earthy vibes to your fingertips. While a classic creamy polish looks great in cinnamon, the shade also offers plenty of opportunities to play around with the finish, like with a dimensional velvet cat eye manicure or a chrome topper.

The latter is one of Stone’s favorite ways to wear the trend—she reaches for Sally Hansen’s Miracle Gel in Chics in Charge to get the effect. “Think the glazed donut’s cousin coming to visit for winter break,” she says. And especially apt for the holidays, you can add a little dazzle to the shade: “If you want to give them some extra sparkle, add a crystal at the base of your nails for an extra catch of light,” she adds.

Stone puts it perfectly: “Every great manicure starts with immaculate cuticle prep and nail care.” Before reaching for any polish, “trim and file your nails to their desired shape,” she says. “Then use Sally Hansen’s Push & Buff Cuticle Pen to clean your nail plate of an excess dead skin.” This helps clean up your cuticles easily and quickly. Next, “wash your hands with soap and water and wipe your nail beds with alcohol,” she adds, noting how important this final prep step is, especially if you’re doing a metallic finish. “Metallic shimmer and polishes expose any texture or dust you may have on your nails,” she explains.

Then, it’s polish time. This part is pretty straightforward: Paint your nails with two coats of your favorite beige or brown nail polish, she says, recommending a medium brown shade “if you want your nail look to be reminiscent of a cinnamon roll” or a reddish brown “if you want your nails more reminiscent of cinnamon as the spice itself.” After letting dry for a few minutes, add top coat and cuticle oil to finish things off. Need some product recs? Stone’s go-to polish is Sally Hansen’s Miracle Gel Polish in Been There, Dune That, but you can also get the look with Olive & June’s Nail Polish in Sassy Molasses or Essie’s Gel Couture Liquid Diamonds in Marquise Please too.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6746. How Does Diplo Survive a Weekend Running, DJing, and Partying? His Whoop Data Tells the Story.

The 47-year-old DJ shared his health stats from a frenzied weekend that could’ve killed any normal person. Here’s how he does it without coming apart at the seams.

On a Saturday morning in August, at exactly 10:00 a.m., August Thomas Wesley Pentz, the DJ known as Diplo, stood outside the Barlume Beach Club in Montauk, Long Island, and delivered a sermon to hundreds of runners. “I’m very tired; I slept three hours for you guys,” he shouted through a megaphone, dressed in gray cargo shorts, a tank top, and pink aviator shades. “But now we’re ready to run. Let’s have a good time.”

Then one of the world’s most prolific DJs launched himself into a 5K through the sand and rocks of Gin Beach. It was a run-club event organized by A Team, a dating app for the wellness obsessed, and Diplo’s Run Club, which stages nine group runs across the U.S. (and, soon, Europe).

As I watched him that sunny and hot morning, I couldn’t stop thinking: How the hell is this guy still standing, let alone leading a run? It was only Saturday, and Diplo was already deep into a frenzied weekend. He landed in New York City on Thursday morning after taking the overnight flight from his home in Malibu. That evening he DJed from 12:30 a.m. to 3:00 a.m. at the Edge, a venue 100 floors above the city. On Friday, he boarded a seaplane for the Hamptons, where he performed two more sets: a sunset show at Surf Lodge, where Jimmy Buffett and Snoop Dogg have played to crowds of models and billionaires, followed by an impromptu set at Mary Lou’s, a Montauk club on the harbor where he only meant to hang out but ended up taking the decks. He didn’t leave until around 1:00 a.m.

I was at both venues and felt deeply, existentially hungover the next morning. I figured—hoped, really—the run would include some walking. Diplo, however, led the pack. He wore a Whoop, the health tracker with which he has a partnership, and reached a heart rate of 167 beats per minute. “He wasn’t taking it easy at all,” said Greg Grosicki, a staff research scientist at Whoop who reviewed Diplo’s data from his weekend in Montauk.

I didn’t understand how a 47-year-old pulled off this feat—the travel, the DJ sets, the cavorting, the running—without collapsing and retreating to his hotel suite for the rest of the weekend. On Saturday evening he had two more sets, one at the annual gala of the Surfrider Foundation, which supports ocean conservation and includes Sean McPherson and John Slattery as supporters. After that he DJed a private party for investment executive Michael Dorrell before flying private to Ibiza overnight for his next gig.

After the run—which, miraculously, I did finish—I talked with Diplo about how he survives this lifestyle. He even shared his Whoop data with me from the weekend so I could compare his body’s reaction with how he told me he felt. “I’m actually fully cracked out every day, doing way more than running and DJing, more than anybody else,” he said.

He likes to sound nonchalant about it. “When I did an interview with some of the Whoop counselors, they were like, your body is very similar mentally to a Navy SEAL or an ER nurse. You’re able to just get up and go do whatever,” he said. “I can always get up and get shit done, unless I’m sick.” Okay, so maybe Diplo was just born with it. But I learned that he does more for his physical and mental health than he likes to let on. This is the story of how a 47-year-old, internationally renowned DJ, who works and parties into the predawn hours, travels the world, and leads packs of people on runs, pulls it off without coming apart at the seams.

Running appeals to him because it travels well. All you need is a pair of shoes and the will to put them on. “I do things I can do anywhere,” he said. “Running is free.” He aims for 15 miles a week across three or four runs, averaging a seven-and-a-half to eight-minute mile. His fastest mile, six and a half minutes, came after his run club appeared on the prediction platform Polymarket, where people bet against him. “That’s what motivated me to run pretty fast,” he said with a laugh.

DJing counts as cardio too. After all, his sets typically run at least two hours. “It’s a mix of dancing and standing,” he said. “It’s almost like a little air squat.” He’s baffled that his dancers, several from the group Major Lazer, stay in shape without a gym. “I’m like, ‘What do you guys do?’ ” he said. “They’re like, ‘We don’t do anything. We just dance at your shows.’ ”

The numbers back him up: During his Surf Lodge set, his max heart rate hit 117 bpm. “If you consider these sets are two hours long, maintaining a heart rate of 120 is a real physical activity,” Greg Grosicki, the research scientist at Whoop, told me. “It’s equivalent to riding a bike for two hours. You feel pretty wrecked after that, and he’s getting equivalent strain from his sets.”

Diplo mixes cardio and weights in HIIT sessions and does 100 to 200 push-ups a day, several times a week, usually in his hotel room. “It takes five minutes of your life,” he said.

Once a week he wears an electrical muscle stimulation, or EMS, suit for 20 minutes. “It does pulsing with simple moves,” he said, naming a brand called Catalyst and a newer one, Pepper, he’s about to try. He credits the suit, half jokingly, for his arm muscles.

He also takes hot Pilates classes with light weights and works out at Barry’s Bootcamp near his home in Malibu. “There’s an unwritten rule about group fitness in California: You don’t bother celebrities,” he said. “You let them get their job done, then you tell people you saw Chris Martin. Every time I go to Pilates, he’s next to me.”

According to the data, long flights, especially red-eyes, hit Diplo hard, said Grosicki. After an overnight flight to Ibiza, Diplo’s recovery score—a measure of how ready the body is to perform—dropped to 21 percent, and his sleep score to 29 percent. That’s well below average for him. “He’s human, and he’s responding to these stressors,” Grosicki said.

“I do like to get the red-eyes, which probably messes up my sleep the most, but I’d rather be outside in the day,” Diplo said. “Being on a plane during the day is so depressing.” He said he struggles to hold a circadian rhythm and sometimes crashes in the midafternoon. On Thursday, for example, he slept five hours in the middle of the day ahead of his set at the Edge.

Winding down after a set is its own challenge. “I might take a Xanax if I have to,” he said. “I got to stop thinking about things.” He also puts his phone away, listens to audiobooks and history podcasts, skips food before bed (though he does chug electrolytes), and uses PEMF mats, which send low-frequency electromagnetic waves through the body to aid in relaxation. Mostly, though, he just takes what sleep he can get.

Saturday morning, after two Friday-night sets in Montauk, his recovery score was 67 percent and his sleep score 65 percent, both above average. On Monday, after he spent the night in Ibiza, his recovery score was 56 percent and sleep score was 62 percent. As Grosicki put it, “He bounces back.”

Diplo avoids drinking before shows and on travel days, and he hasn’t done recreational drugs in six months. When he does, it’s acid. “I would run on acid, just try things on acid,” he said. “I could fly a plane on acid.”

“Mental health is pretty much everything,” Diplo said. “But you have to get there sometimes by exercising. Finding your body, being in tune with your body, is probably the first thing you can do to have a clear mind, to get rid of any sickness you have, whatever’s keeping you down. That’s the first way to start.”

He’s tried more unconventional resets too. Seven years ago, he smoked toad venom. “I’ll never do it again, but it was pretty hardcore,” he said. “It’s a really big ego death. Being humbled is very good wellness.”

Whatever he’s doing, it seems to be working, at least by the numbers. Whether the rest of us should try it is a different question entirely.

#6742. The Saga of Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s Fake Leather Jacket

The Internet loved his outrageous new look. There was just one problem.

There are several ways to respond when you’ve admitted to an affair, your company’s shares are tumbling, and even the White House is discussing your private life. Turning up in a spectacularly studded Saint Laurent leather jacket is one of them. Resigning is another.

For a while, it seemed as if Jensen Huang, the 63-year-old former CEO of Nvidia, had opted for the former. A photograph showing the tech billionaire in an archival Hedi Slimane YSL biker jacket went viral this week. The Internet was delighted. There was just one problem: The photograph was fake.

The resignation, which came just this morning, was real.

As CNN reported, the image had been doctored from a genuine photograph taken at a Seoul airport in June. Nvidia, which one might imagine has other things to deal with right now, confirmed it wasn’t real.

But perhaps the reason so many people believed it was that Huang already dresses like this. Leather jackets are his uniform. He reportedly owns 30 or 40 of them, including a snazzy reptile-effect number worn to deliver his company’s keynote at the Consumer Electronics Show.

And Hedi Slimane was the perfect designer to bring into the fantasy. The Frenchman, who ran Saint Laurent from 2012 to 2016, made his name dressing skinny rock stars and turning their disheveled, devil-may-care attitude—later rebranded as so-called “indie sleaze”—into expensive luxury fashion. His leather jackets were practically a uniform for men who were determined to look as though they didn’t care what anyone thought and/or having a midlife crisis.

For a billionaire in the middle of a spectacularly public scandal, it was quite a look. One Instagram commenter described Huang as the first person to achieve “negative aura” in archival Hedi.

There’s another irony. Huang has previously said his wife, Lori—i.e., the wronged party in all this—helps choose his clothes.

And then there’s the technology itself. Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution, including the tools capable of ... producing convincing fake photographs.

In conclusion, then, the image wasn’t real. But as a portrait of a billionaire in the middle of a spectacularly public crisis, we so wanted it to be.

#6741. ‘DWTS’ Frontrunner Amber Glenn Has Had to Make a Major Decision to Stay in the Competition

Competing for the Mirrorball means making some tough choices off the dance floor.

We all know Amber Glenn is a fierce competitor, thanks to her Olympic gold medals. So, it’s no surprise she’s one of the semifinalists on Dancing With the Stars season 35. But what fans might not know is how much she’s given up to keep pushing toward the Mirrorball trophy.

According to The Sun, Amber has stepped back from figure skating events to focus on the beloved reality competition. She was most recently removed from the entry list for the NHK Trophy in Japan, which kicks off later this month. It’s the sixth and final event in the Grand Prix regular season and the second figure skating event she’s opted out of at the last minute.

Amber also withdrew from this week’s Saatva Skate America competition in Everett, Washington, as she preps for the DWTS semifinals.

“For now, Amber is focused solely on DWTS, which has taken up most of her energy and time with grueling rehearsals and weekly live shows,” the outlet reports.

The Olympian is a frontrunner for this year’s coveted prize, up against Maura Higgins, Connor Wood, Harry Shum Jr., Ezra Frech, and Tatyana Ali.

During a November 12 appearance on The Zach Sang Show, Amber opened up about what it would mean for her to help her pro partner, Pasha Pashkov, win his first-ever Mirrorball trophy.

“That would mean the world because, and I’ve said this before, is that I came into this competition like, of course, who doesn’t want to win?” she said. “But it wasn’t like, ‘I need this because I’ve worked my entire life for it.’ Like, I’ve been dancing for two months. Yes, it has been a very intense, very passionate two months of working, but it has been his whole life that he has been dancing.”

She continued, “Imagine how much work I’m putting into it. He’s doing twice that amount because he has to choreograph. He has to do so many, like, visual selections. Like, there’s so much that the pro does for every single show that people don’t see.So yes, it’s about the celeb and who wins, but it also is about the pro.”

Though Amber has had to give up some skating for ballroom dancing, there’s a chance she’ll be back on the ice at the Prevagen U.S. Championships later this season in January.

In the meantime, we can’t wait to see her crush the DWTS semifinals next week!

#6739. Rosalía Wins With Her Style at the 2026 Latin Grammy Awards

She wore a quartet of designer looks from Matières Fécales, Erdem, Vivienne Westwood, and Dior

Rosalía won big at the 27th annual Latin Grammy Awards last night. For her fourth studio album, Lux, the Spanish pop singer took home her third trophy for Album of the Year, becoming the first woman in the history of the ceremony to ever do so. She collected a total of five awards, coming out on top in other major categories like Song of the Year, which was awarded to her track “La Perla” (featuring Yahritza y Su Esencia), and Best Short Form Music Video, which went to her visual for “Sauvignon Blanc.” And to coincide with such a major night, Rosalía brought out a few major designer looks, all styled by Chloe & Chenelle Delgadillo.

To start the evening, the “Berghain” singer hit the red carpet in the avant-garde label of the moment, Matières Fécales, which has been embraced by everyone from Zendaya to Rihanna and Demi Moore.

Courtesy of the label’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection, Rosalía’s angelic white gown featured a ruched tulle bodice with semi-sheer folds that hugged her body and culminated in the house’s signature déchiré detailing. The tattered fragments arched from Rosalía’s shoulders like wings, while also dangling from the ends of her cropped sleeves. Down low, there was equal drama courtesy of a dramatic tulle bubble skirt that trailed around her in a voluminous cascade. Of course, her signature halo-dyed hair acted as the perfect complement to the heavenly ensemble.

Later, Rosalía hit the stage to perform a medley of songs from Lux, including “La Perla,” “La Rumba del Perdón,” and “Dios Es un Stalker.” For her performance, she wore a bespoke satin gown from Erdem, pulled from the house’s Fall/Winter 2026 collection. (Just last month, she turned to the same London-based label for a stunning balletcore street-style moment.)

The golden-hued design featured a corset-style bodice and knotted bows along the neckline, with a basque waist and flared ruffle skirt that was overlayed with black tulle and trimmed with ostrich feathers.

Once she returned to her seat, it turned out Rosalía had another surprise look in store, as she changed into a custom white dress from Vivienne Westwood. The bridal-inspired design featured a strapless, plunging sweetheart bodice decorated with a tiny bow in the middle, while layers of fabric were draped along the waist for a subtle peplum effect.

All of the fabric, meanwhile, was embroidered with floral lace details and microscopic pearl embellishments, and the singer wore a scarf to match, while slipping into a pair of contrasting black pumps.

To finish her quartet of looks, Rosalía switched into a custom black Dior dress by Jonathan Anderson, who, fittingly, also helped design many of the looks for the Spanish superstar’s recent Lux Tour.

The silky black slip featured two delicate lace straps that hung off Rosalía’s shoulders, while the bust tied into a knot that dangled in front like a tassel. The top half of the dress was decorated with subtle floral details, while the skirt featured panels of semi-sheer material. It all worked perfectly to serve as an inky backdrop as Rosalía posed with her stack of gramophone trophies.

As she accepted her prize for Album of the Year, Rosalía got teary-eyed, saying: “This is the third time I’ve won this award. If El Mal Querer was a dream, and Motomami was a bold gamble, then Lux has been a leap into the unknown. I believe it’s very important to speak your own truth—and for others to be able to speak theirs—and this record is mine. I thank God, and I thank my loved ones: my family and my friends. Thank you for your love and for shaping me into who I am today. The truth is, I know I still have a lot to learn, and I’m certain that I likely haven’t even created my best work yet.”

#6738. The 17 Best Comedy Movies of 2026

Thanks to The Invite, Coyote vs. Acme, and Stop! That! Train!, big-screen comedy is making a comeback.

Are comedies making a comeback? Over the past decade, you couldn’t get a laugh into theaters even if it was forced. Hollywood decided that comedies weren’t worth the same treatment as superhero tentpoles or low-budget horror, and just about every legacy franchise sequel of a former ‘90s comedy disappointed audiences because they were basically cash grabs that played on nostalgia. Comedy needed help.

So, in 2026, some fresh films forced Hollywood to come to its senses. Coyote vs. Acme, a Looney Tunes film that Warner Bros. originally dropped to claim a tax write-off, grossed over $126 million at the box office. Olivia Wilde’s The Invite might just receive nominations for every major award category at the Oscars, and Airplane!-esque humor made a comeback in RuPaul’s hilarious disaster flick Stop! That! Train!

If you’re in need of a belly laugh, check out the best comedies of 2026 below.

Don’t get me wrong, Coyote vs. Acme is no Space Jam. But the Looney Tunes film that triumphantly won the box office—even after Warner Bros. gave up on it—is still one of the funniest movies of the year. The story follows Wile E. Coyote as he partners with a lowly injury attorney (Will Forte) to sue the ACME Corporation for its defective products. The comedy also stars John Cena as ACME’s defense attorney, who probably has the funniest line of the year when he tells Tweety Bird: “I don’t pay you to tink you taw, I pay you to taw.”

Olivia Colman stars in this romantic comedy drama about a lonely fisherwoman who grows tired of the town’s harassment due to her smell. So, she asks a magical basket weaver (Peter Dinklage) to make her a husband (Alexander Skarsgård) out of wicker. What follows is a bizarre yet hilarious story about the follies of marriage, jealousy, and tons of sex with a man made out of basket material.

Sam Rockwell leads this sci-fi comedy by former Pirates of the Caribbean director Gore Verbinski, about a time-traveler who travels to the same diner over and over again in a Groundhog Day–esque loop because he believes that if he can take the right configuration of people from the diner back to the future with him then he may be able to save the world from AI’s takeover. I know, it’s strange. But Verbinski makes the comedy work, especially when you learn about each diner patron via small vignettes emphasizing various doomsday scenarios.

Real estate agent Rudd Landy (John Cena) is called to the hospital when his brother gets into a car accident. Turns out, the man in emergency care is not his real brother (Christopher Meloni), but someone that he used to mentor when he was younger as part of a Big Brothers Big Sisters program. Without anywhere else to go, Rudd agrees to help take care of his “little brother” (Eric André) while he recovers, even if his little brother might just ruin his life.

When Gail Daughtry’s (Zoey Deutch) husband takes their celebrity sex pass joke literally, Gail sets out to get even by tracking down her celebrity sex pass—John Hamm—to have sex with him and even out the score. The comedy film also stars “Weird Al” Yankovic, Ben Wang, Sabrina Impacciatore, Joe Lo Truglio, Fred Melamed, and Mad Men’s John Slattery as himself.

Johnny Knoxville, Steve-O, and the whole Jackass crew return for one last ride in Jackass: Best and Last, another compilation of skits, stunts, and pranks from some of the most fearless people on the planet. They hit each other in the groin with wooden mallets, hook their penises up to shock collars, and reminisce about some of their most iconic sketches throughout the years.

Half RuPaul’s Drag Race, half Airplane! for Bravo fans, Stop! That! Train! is the dumbest movie I’ve seen all year (complimentary). The film follows RuPaul and her Drag Race stars as they attempt to stop a speeding bullet train heading straight toward the storm of the century, aka a Stormaganza. Even if you’re so out of your depth that you have no idea what’s going on, Stop! That! Train! argues that that’s okay. It’s comedy for comedy’s sake, and we need more of that at the theater.

Mel Brooks just turned 100 years old (!!!). But before that, he was 99 in Mel Brooks: The 99-Year-Old Man!—a two-part HBO documentary directed by Judd Apatow. The film, which chronicles the comedian’s career throughout The Producers, Blazing Saddles, Spaceballs, Young Frankenstein, and many more, also touches on his work as a film producer. Still, it’s the testimonies from his friendship with Carl Reiner and his marriage to Anne Bancroft that really pull at the heartstrings after a good laugh down memory lane.

Olivia Wilde is not afraid of Virginia Woolf. In the actress’s latest film, The Invite, the classic dinner-party setup full of playful talking and relationship arguments somehow morphs into a sex comedy. The whole film takes place in Angela (Wilde) and Joe’s (Seth Rogen) apartment as they welcome the raucous upstairs neighbors Pina (Penelope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton). But unlike Who’s Afraid of Virginia Woolf? and its descent into anger and grief, The Invite asks more questions about love, commitment, and everyone’s stance on orgies.

The struggling music duo always works for me. Whether it’s Flight of the Conchords or Ishtar, there’s something funny about people with no talent still trying to make it to the top. Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie takes the genre to maybe its lowest budget yet, as the Canadian web-series comedians somehow also make room to spoof Back to the Future and incorporate real Nathan Fielder–like guerrilla filmmaking.

One of the coolest movies to premiere out of SXSW was the Hulu original Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice, from writer/director BenDavid Grabinski. A loan shark (Vince Vaughn) uses a time machine to undo one bad night involving his close friend (James Marsden) and his wife (Eiza González). Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice is the kind of movie you want when the pizza is hot, the beers are cold, and your friends are indecisive about what you all want to watch.

After years of creative and financial slump, Pixar has found its groove again with Hoppers. Essentially Animal Farm meets Avatar, Hoppers tells the story of a genius college student whose participation in a breakthrough science experiment lets her become a beaver who can communicate with animals. Though her efforts to help animals seem promising at first, she quickly realizes what really happens when animals (and bugs!) fight back against mankind. Directed by We Bare Bears creator Daniel Chong, Hoppers is an easygoing, family-friendly romp through the wilderness.

Liam Neeson continues his funny streak in the action comedy Cold Storage. Joe Keery (Stranger Things) plays a slacker employee of a storage facility who, along with his cute coworker (Georgina Campbell), discovers a deadly, mutating fungus. Teaming up with a grizzled federal agent (Neeson), the three work to save the world. Basically a modern-day Outbreak, Cold Storage isn’t one to leave dormant on your watch list.

Dave Bautista and Jason Momoa make up the ultimate action-movie tag team in The Wrecking Crew. The guys play estranged brothers in Hawaii who work together to piece together who killed their father and how on earth the Japanese yakuza is involved.

Boots Riley, the writer and director of Sorry to Bother You, does it again with his crime caper I Love Boosters. In this anticapitalist satire, a crew of professional thieves works to take down a ruthless fashion giant (Demi Moore). Keke Palmer, Naomi Ackie, Taylour Paige, Poppy Liu, Eiza González, and more make up this delirious ensemble that takes Ocean’s Eleven and dresses it in hyper-surreal shades.

Sometimes we need a nauseatingly cute rom-com. Kevin James (yes, The King of Queens) stars in this breezy and uplifting comedy about a man who, after being left at the altar, goes on his Italian honeymoon solo. While there, he makes new friends and even deeper connections with strangers halfway around the world. A feel-good flick about new beginnings, Solo Mio can lift you high when you’re feeling down.

A stoner comedy? In 2026? In this economy? Well, the streaming generation can finally know what it’s like to have their own Harold & Kumar with Pizza Movie. Gaten Matarazzo and Sean Giambrone star as college roommates and social pariahs who, while high on a new hallucinogenic drug, embark on a psychedelic odyssey to make it downstairs and get their pizza delivery. Despite a popular meme mocking the movie’s marketing, Pizza Movie performed well with critics at its SXSW premiere.
